Overwatch doesn’t have ‘any intention’ of making 6-vs-6 the main mode, but it’s now ‘a second among equals’

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

The never-ending battle between Overwatch players choosing five-vs-five or six-vs-six will rage on.

In a new Director’s Take blog today, game director Aaron Keller confirmed that Overwatch will continue to support both formats, but with a renewed focus on the classic six-vs-six, which the studio is “starting to think of it as a second among equals.”

“We always say that we want to make the game you all want to play and it’s obvious that we have folks who want different things,” Keller said, citing player surveys that were very split between the two options, as seen above. “Over the past few years, we’ve realized that there doesn’t have to be a ‘winner’ between five-vs-five and six-vs-six. The real competition should be in your matches, not between two modes that can coexist. You can play either one (many people actually play both!) and we’re going to support that choice.”

Personally? I’m a fan of six-vs-six, but that’s because I’m a tank main. Solo tanking is a pain compared to having a buddy on the frontline with you, and I can see that there are many who agree with me…but also many who don’t. Overwatch will continue to feature both, Keller says, and there won’t be any radical switches soon either, other than an additional six-vs-six queue.

“Six-vs-six has been slowly growing in popularity over the last few years; however, the five-vs-five version of Open Queue was popular too,” Keller said. “We’d like to learn how many people are playing Open Queue six-vs-six for the role flexibility and how many people are playing it for the team size. Running both modes at the same time should produce some good data points to show us where we ought to be putting more energy.”

Keller warned that adding the new queue (Role Queue six-vs-six) instead of replacing an old one may impact queue times, but it “should be better overall for our community.” He also said that the team theorizes that some matchmaking changes coming in season six “will keep queue times more manageable” alongside the new option.

“We’ll put more energy into balance patches for [six-vs-six] and we’ll continue to look for the right format that really gives our community the experience they’re looking for,” Keller said. “Whether that’s Open Queue, Role Queue, a combination of both, or a new format that we’ve yet to try out, we’re here to figure out what works best for you.”

Senior Staff Writer. Professional writer for over 10 years. Lover of all things Marvel, Metal Gear, Final Fantasy, Resident Evil, and more. Previous bylines include PC Gamer, Red Bull Esports, Fanbyte, and Esports Nation. DogDad to 2 corgis, sports fan (NY Yankees, NY Jets, NY Rangers, NY Knicks), Paramore and K-pop fanatic, cardio enthusiast.

Learnosity is a B2B company providing assessment infrastructure to the publishers, platforms, and product teams serving the global learning industry.

Its flagship product, Learnosity Build, brings Learnosity’s assessment APIs together in one API-first engine that covers the whole assessment lifecycle: authoring, item health, delivery, scoring, feedback and reporting. Customers keep their own UX, content and roadmap. Build handles the assessment underneath, along with the scale, security, privacy and accessibility that come with it.

The numbers: 47 million learners, 1.02 billion assessments and 24 billion questions delivered a year.

Build is designed for every team that works on assessment. Now, let’s check out some of those teams.

AI-assisted authoring helps content teams create more without sacrificing quality, across more than 50 question types. Item Bank Health Check uses AI to find and fix issues across an entire item bank, from missing alt text to cultural sensitivity and language gaps, keeping content accurate, aligned, and accessible.

Engineers built on one API-first engine for the whole lifecycle: integrate once, deliver everywhere. Build is agent-ready, so teams can work with it directly from AI coding tools like Cursor, Claude, and Copilot. That leaves them free to spend their time on the features that make their product distinctive.

Operations teams get assessment that runs at the standard their institutions require: burst-ready autoscaling for peak periods, SLA-backed uptime, round-the-clock monitoring, ISO 27001 certification, and compliance with FERPA, COPPA, GDPR and WCAG 2.2 AA.

Learnosity’s AI work also covers grading and feedback. Feedback Aide provides accurate AI scoring and written feedback, from short answers to extended essays, at any volume. Learnosity Math is an AI-enriched math engine that can generate question variants and grade open-ended responses. Teams can choose manual, AI, or adaptive grading, aligned to their rubrics and always under human control.

Whatever the team, the model is the same: Learnosity handles the assessment infrastructure, so its customers can focus on the learning experiences that set them apart.

Sofia Franklyn Questions If Her Friendship with Alex Cooper Was Ever Real

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Sofia Franklyn is questioning whether her friendship with Alex Cooper was ever real ... six years after their explosive "Call Her Daddy" breakup.

The podcaster opened up about her former co-host during Tuesday's episode of Amanda Hirsch's "Not Skinny But Not Fat" podcast ... admitting she still has doubts about the friendship they shared before their very public falling-out.

"Obviously, I question, like, was the friendship ever real?" Sofia said, adding ... "Maybe it was real for her in how she thinks friendships should be. I don't know."

Sofia revealed the two never had a conversation to hash things out after their 2020 split ... and she still seems stunned by how quickly everything unraveled.

She recalled learning Alex had struck a deal with Dave Portnoy's Barstool Sports behind her back ... saying she was in complete shock as she watched her best friend move forward with the show without her.

"This is my best friend," Sofia recalled thinking at the time ... adding what really upset her was what happened afterward.

Sofia pointed to Alex's first episodes without her, which she and Amanda characterized as a "funeral" for Sofia's time on the show ... with Sofia agreeing Alex was essentially "murdering" her publicly.

The former co-hosts parted ways in 2020 amid a bitter contract dispute with Barstool Sports. Alex stayed with "Call Her Daddy," while Sofia launched her own podcast, "Sofia with an F."

The latest remarks come as Sofia releases her new memoir, "Daddy Issues" ... in which she revisits the messy breakup and shares her side of the story six years later.

And judging by her latest comments ... she's still wondering whether she ever really knew her former best friend.

AMD Zen 6 EPYC ‘Venice’ deep dive and benchmarking

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Our first look at Zen 6, both under the hood and in real-world applications.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

AMD’s EPYC ‘Venice’ CPUs are the first to debut the new Zen 6 architecture, and we’ve been testing two chips from the range over the past several weeks to see how they compare to Nvidia’s Vera, and more broadly, how the Zen 6 architecture departs from Zen 5 ahead of the expected consumer-focused Olympic Ridge launch next year. Zen 5 was a ground-up redesign of AMD’s storied Zen core, and Zen 6 takes that foundation and expands it in just about every regard.

Zen 6 uses TSMC’s N2P node, but it also comes with significant architectural changes under the hood. Within the core, a redesigned vector engine pushes AMD’s SIMD performance even higher, and it now natively supports half-precision FP16 instructions alongside full, 32-bit precision. It also splits integer schedulers within the execution engine, allowing for more instructions in flight through the core.

Across the die, AMD made other significant changes. The single IOD of previous generations is now split across two IODs, giving the SP7 platform access to 16 memory channels (though at the cost of PCIe 6.0 lanes compared to the previous generation and SP8 platform).

The CCDs have been upgraded, too, for the first time since Zen 3. A standard Zen 6 CCD can now scale up to 12 cores, while a Zen 6c packs 32 cores. With Zen 5 and 5c, CCDs topped out at eight cores and 16 cores, respectively. For the Zen 6c CCD, AMD also doubled the amount of shared L3 per core, which effectively quadruples the L3 compared to Zen 5c, since the CCD packs twice the number of cores.

Interestingly, Zen 6 is neither a marginal optimization to Zen 5, nor a complete architecture redesign. Stepping back, it looks like AMD pushes as many dials as it has access to to their highest level, and that holds up looking at performance.

The 256-core EPYC 9996 delivers 1,024 threads in a 2P configuration, which came out 93% ahead of the 768-thread 2P EPYC 9965 server we tested in SPEC CPU 2026’s intrate throughput test. The frequency-optimized EPYC 9686F excels elsewhere, notably in scientific computing and HPC workloads, and it serves as AMD’s competitor to Vera, at least before the LPDDR-based Verano platform arrives from AMD next year.

AMD says it believes Venice is fundamentally a generation ahead of the competition, which would be a worthless line if it wasn’t backed up by data. Intel’s Granite Rapids-AP is rarely in the conversation, and although Vera maintains some competitive positions, Venice is often ahead — sometimes orders of magnitude ahead.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Intel’s Diamond Rapids is set to launch next year, which should help balance the scales. But AMD has set an incredibly high bar with Venice, one that it will likely extend even higher for some workloads with the arrival of Venice-X and Verano next year.

There’s a lot to get to here. We have a full deep dive into the Zen 6 microarchitecture, a broader look at the chip itself, the platform(s) AMD is pushing out this generation, and of course, more than 100 benchmarks covering everything from Python performance to HPC.

The headline for Zen 6 is a 27% average IPC uplift over Zen 5 in enterprise, cloud, and AI workloads. The debut of this new microarchitecture is focused on the data center, so you shouldn’t take that 27% IPC improvement as broadly applicable to all workloads. Additionally, AMD arrived at that number by comparing fixed-frequency Zen 5 to Zen 6c with 16 cores. As we’ll get into in the next section, a Zen 6c CCD can comfortably fit 16 Zen 6c cores with room to spare, while 16 Zen 5 cores are split across two CCDs.

The compute die is built on TSMC’s N2P node, which is an already optimized version of N2 that comes with a 5% performance improvement. Compared to N3E (Zen 5 used N4P), N2P offers an 18% speed improvement at iso-power, or a 36% reduction in power at the same speed.

AMD described Zen 5 as a ground-up redesign when the microarchitecture first broke cover in mid-2024, characterized by a wider execution engine capable of dispatching and returning eight instructions per cycle, a wider frontend to feed it, full AVX-512 support, and doubled bandwidth between the L2 and L1 cache. Zen 6 is a continuation of the redesign we saw on Zen 5. It carries many of the same characteristics, as well as the same general topology, just enhanced in a number of ways.

As we go through the breakdown, keep in mind that Zen 6 and Zen 6c use the same microarchitecture. Zen 6c is just a space-optimized design that consumes less power, clocks lower, and, obviously, takes up less die space than a full Zen 6 core. But, the company uses the same microarchitecture inside the core, unlike Intel’s E-cores, which use a different microarchitecture than the P-cores.

Starting with the frontend, it’s largely unchanged from Zen 5, short of a few notable improvements. You get the same dual 4-wide decode pipes and 8-dispatch, but AMD has improved the BPU and op cache. For the BPU, it has a 64-entry Return Address Stack, up from 52 on Zen 5. AMD also increased the op cache size back up to 6.75K entries from 6K on Zen 5 (Zen 4 also had a 6.75K-entry op cache). This larger cache can serve up to 18 micro-ops per cycle, up from 12 on Zen 5.

AMD split the integer schedulers for Zen 6 (Zen 5 had a unified scheduler), now offering higher capacity, with 96 shared ALU/AGU and 48 dedicated ALU entries, compared to an 88 / 56 split for ALU/AGU on Zen 5. AMD also expanded the general-purpose registers to a 352-entry physical register file, up from 240 on Zen 5.

The vector execution engine is also improved, following on the heels of a true 512-bit register in Zen 5. Zen 6 carries forward that 512-bit data path and adds some additional improvements. Notably, the engine now supports half-precision AVX-512 FP16 instructions, which needed to be converted in Zen 5’s vector execution engine. Also notable is the FP load/store bandwidth. Zen 5 supports two loads of any size, but Zen 6 supports either two 512-bit loads or four 256-bit loads. Store bandwidth is unchanged, at one 512-bit store or two 256-bit stores.

Zen 6 carries forward the 48 KB 12-way L1 data cache from Zen 5, along with 1 MB 16-way private L2. The shared L3 is treated as victim cache for the L2, as is the case in Zen 5. However, AMD increased the in-flight window compared to Zen 5, with an 80-entry load queue and 128-entry store queue.

Perhaps the biggest change going from Zen 5 to Zen 6 comes in the density of the CCD. Zen 6 offers up to 12 cores per CCD, up from 8 on Zen 5, while Zen 6c scales up to 32, double that of Zen 5c. Elsewhere, the updated CCDs either match or improve on Zen 5/5c CCDs.

For Zen 6, AMD retained the 4 MB of L3 cache per core, increasing the total cache to 48 MB to accommodate the new 12-core limit. The maximum frequency of a standard Zen 6 CCD has increased from 5 GHz on Zen 5 to 5.3 GHz on Zen 6. Keep in mind these clocks are in the context of Venice; we expect higher maximum boost clocks on consumer Olympic Ridge.

Zen 6c has more enhancements. AMD doubled the amount of L3 per core from 2 MB to 4 MB, matching the normal Zen 6 CCD. And, with double the L3 per core and double the number of cores, the Zen 6c CCD has a total of 128 MB of L3, quadruple that of a Zen 5c CCD. For Zen 6c, the maximum frequency is also up, going from 3.7 GHz on Zen 5c to 4.1 GHz on Zen 6c.

Across both, AMD claims a “large core IPC uplift,” as well as improved power and SMT efficiency. We haven’t seen an overall IPC claim yet, though the numbers referenced above put Venice at about a 27% advantage over Turin in enterprise, cloud, and AI workloads.

In the context of Venice, AMD can fit up to eight CCDs on a chip, coming out to 256 cores in total for Zen 6c, and 96 cores total for regular Zen 6 for SP7 platforms (the initial lineup for Venice). AMD is moving past the SP5 socket available to Genoa and Turin CPUs for SP7, which is a slightly larger socket at 76 x 80 mm (SP5 is ~72 x 75 mm).

The topology of the chip is largely intact from Turin, short of the dual IO dies that characterize Venice. The two IODs live in the middle of the chip, flanked by Zen 6 CCDs. The second IOD is what gives SP7 chips access to 16 channels of memory per socket (8 channels per IOD), supporting up to 12,800 MT/s speeds using MRDIMMs in a 1DPC configuration. With RDIMMs, AMD supports up to 8,000 MT/s. The platform supports up to 8 TB of memory per socket, and AMD claims bandwidth of up to 1.6 TB/s per socket (1 TB/s per socket with RDIMMs).

Also on the IOD, there’s the hardware root-of-trust, as well as AMD’s confidential computing extensions, including execution encryption, memory integrity, and trusted I/O. New to the platform is PCIe 6.0 at speeds up to 64 Gbps per lane. A 1P SP7 configuration has access to 96 PCIe 6.0 lanes, while a 2P platform has up to 128. Each configuration also comes with “bonus” PCIe 4.0 lanes, either 8 for 1P configs or 16 for 2P.

Two 64G SerDes interfaces bring the PCIe lanes into the IOD, one of which is used to link the chips in a 2P configuration. That second interface can still optionally handle an additional 12 PCIe 6.0 lanes, giving the “up to” 128 2P lanes number. The bonus lanes run through a smaller 16G SerDes interface.

Platforms with the SP8 socket trade memory channels for PCIe lanes. The SP8 socket is smaller at 62.5 x 80 mm, and the platform supports eight memory channels instead of 16, though with the same support for up to 12,800 MT/s MRDIMMs. Memory bandwidth drops to 819 GB/s for MRDIMMs and 512 GB/s for RDIMMs at the maximum supported transfer rate.

However, SP8 chips have access to 64 PCIe 6.0 lanes per IOD, for a total of 128 lanes in a socket (plus the additional eight PCIe 4.0 bonus lanes). Just like SP7, some of those lanes are shared in a 2P configuration, dropping the total to 192 PCIe 6.0 lanes in 2P SP8 servers, with 16 additional PCIe 4.0 lanes. Both platforms support CXL 3.1.

AMD also provided a platform breakdown for its upcoming Verano range, which uses the Zen 6 microarchitecture on an LPDDR5X-based platform, leveraging replaceable SOCAMM2 DIMMs.

AMD has added a few quality-of-life features to Venice CPUs that should make them easier to manage when actually deployed. First is UPP, or Unified Power Provisioning, which is a power management feature that takes into account total platform power, allocating it as needed to different components, depending on the workload. With it, AMD says it’s able to deliver a power consumption of 830W on 16 servers to 706W on 18 servers, or a decrease in provisioned power by 15%, while increasing the number of servers per rack.

Also dealing with power is UBPS, or Utilization Based Power Scaling. It’s a dynamic power scaling feature that aligns with whatever workload is running on the server, which AMD says “helps close the gap between provisioned and consumed power.” Like UPP, UBPS is concerned with platform power, not just the power consumption of the CPU.

The most interesting addition is FAST, or Frequency Adaptive Spectrum Technology. The feature takes in telemetry from the platform and policies set in firmware to adjust the frequency within set power and thermal limits. It sounds a little more involved than a simple Precision Boost Overdrive for the data center, however.

AMD described FAST to us as allowing for two performance tiers within one socket. As an example, AMD pointed to a 96-core Venice CPU, saying FAST allows 32 of those cores to run in a “premium” category, with a base clock of 3.65 GHz and a max clock of 5 GHz, while a “batch” category with 64 cores runs at 2 GHz with a 2.4 GHz max. AMD described it as multiple commercial SKUs within a single socket.

Venice marks the point when AMD will start offering MEXT on its EPYC platforms. AMD aquired MEXT earlier this year, and it has used the feature internally. With HPE, AMD is rolling out “MEXT ready” platforms, which will, eventually, have access to MEXT licenses. The technology is interesting, especially given how constrained memory is right now.

The general concept of MEXT is using flash as DRAM. Even the fastest flash is orders of magnitude slower than DRAM, but MEXT attempts to take advantage of DRAM access patterns to expose more memory to the system without it actually being there. Pages that haven’t been accessed in several minutes are offloaded to flash storage, and an AI model predicts pages that are needed. Then, those pages are loaded back into memory as you need them.

Conceptually, it sounds too good to be true. AMD pointed out that MEXT is using a transformer-based LLM, and effectively, LLMs are predicting what words go next when generating text. Here, AMD says the bar isn’t even that high. As long as the model can stream the correct pages in, and do so fast enough to cover the flash latency, the order of them doesn’t matter.

MEXT is delivered through a Linux kernel module, and AMD has bold claims about its capabilities. The company has deployed the software internally on 145 servers, which it expects to expand to over 2,000 servers next year. Currently, AMD is using it for critical EDA workflows, such as Synopsis PrimeTime. The company says that it’s able to maintain 95% of the application’s performance on Venice with just 50% of the system DRAM. Going down as low as 25%, the company claims the app maintains 80% of its performance.

We’ll have more on MEXT shortly. The feature is exclusive to EPYC CPUs, and AMD was clear that it only applies to certain workloads right now. There are likely applications where you won’t be able to cut the DRAM capacity of a server, or where you’ll need to use a more conservative cut of 25% or less.

AMD gave us access to two Zen 6 Venice systems for the purpose of testing, both of which were dual-socket servers. For the first system, we tested the dense EPYC 9996, which is AMD’s high-core-count design, packing 256 cores and 512 threads per socket, for a total of 512 cores and 1024 threads across a 2P system. It’s a peak throughput SKU, designed to handle heavily concurrent workloads.

On the other end of the spectrum, we have the EPYC 9686F, which is still a high core count CPU in its own right, with 96 cores and 192 threads, giving a 2P system 192 cores and 384 threads. The “F” suffix for AMD’s data center CPUs notes a high-frequency SKU, designed to push performance up in more demanding applications, as opposed to the dense, frontend-focused EPYC 9996.

Both chips use AMD’s SP7 socket, which is geared toward data centers, with higher core count SKUs. The upcoming SP8 socket is focused on enterprise customers, with smaller core counts and support for fewer memory channels.

Although the EPYC 9996 is a dense design with the Zen 6c core, it comes with a fairly high boost clock of 4.1 GHz, though a much lower 2.55 GHz base clock, which it will likely be running closer to in real deployments. It comes with a massive 1,024 MB pool of L3 for the chip, as well as a 600W rated TDP, though it can be manually adjusted between 400W and 600W.

The EPYC 9686F proves out AMD’s claims about Venice, serving as one of two chips in the SP7 lineup to boost up to 5 GHz. It has a lower 500W TDP, though it can again run between 400W and 600W. This is the SKU AMD has heavily referenced in its published Venice benchmarks as a “96-core HF” model.

It’s an interesting model, perhaps more so than the EPYC 9996. It’s in direct competition with Nvidia’s Vera CPU, not only based on core count, but also on performance. These two chips trade blows in several benchmarks, and it’s clear that AMD is pushing this SKU as its “agentic CPU” of the Venice lineup, similar to how Nvidia has positioned Vera.

32 x 64 GB (2TB) SK hynix DDR5-12800 ECC MRDIMM

24 x 64 GB (1.5 TB) SK hynix DDR5-6000 ECC RDIMM

24 x 64GB (1.5 TB) Micron DDR5-8800 ECC MRDIMM

16 x 96 GB (1.5 TB) Samsung LPDDR5-9600 ECC SOCAMM2

You can see the platforms we tested above. The Nigeria reference platform we tested these Zen 6 chips on came with top-end DDR5-12800 MRDIMMs, arranged to fully saturate the 16-channel memory with 32 sticks. This massive jump in memory support is a critical aspect of Venice, particularly for the hotly contested EPYC 9686F and its all-important matchup against Vera.

Vera’s use of LPDDR5 SOCAMM2 memory allows for higher transfer rates than standard RDIMMs in a field-replaceable package, though through a relatively tame eight-channel setup. Vera can get away with 8-channel memory, however, with its topology allowing for massive data movement across the chip. AMD’s distributed design necessitates a bigger memory configuration, with double the channels and access to even higher transfer rates through the use of MRDIMMs.

Starting off our benchmarks is SPEC CPU 2026, the de facto industry benchmark for server CPU performance. As usual with SPEC runs, the numbers below are “estimates.” We gathered the numbers from real SPEC runs, but we haven’t reported them to the SPEC committee, so they remain “estimates.”

For the throughput benchmark suites, intrate and fprate, we ran a number of copies for each application equal to the thread count of the chip. All of our tests were run on Ubuntu Server 26.04 with Linux Kernel 7.0, and we used GCC 15.2 for all but the Venice chips. GCC 16 brings official support for Zen 6, which we used here. However, we did regression testing against GCC 15 and found only minor differences, and nothing that would change our standings.

Using GCC 16 was important to keep our optimization flags equal. We used common GCC flags, including fast link-time optimization and march/mcpu flags to expose the correct processor instruction support. We also used the open-source jemalloc memory allocator across all tested systems.

The overall intrate scores set the tone, particularly for the EPYC 9996. It has access to 1,024 threads in a 2P configuration, multiplying the clear speed improvements in Zen 6 broadly across more threads compared to last-gen’s top-end EPYC 9965. The 9996 scored a massive 93% higher in this test, though that number requires some context.

Both throughput benchmarks in SPEC reward not only how much work is done, but also how quickly it’s done. With access to a much larger thread pool and improvements in Zen 6 broadly, there’s that multiplicative effect when looking at pure throughput. A 2P EPYC 9996 system is 93% faster than a 2P EPYC 9965 system in this benchmark, but not broadly across the board.

The EPYC 9686F provides some context, which is still good news for AMD, just not on the level of doubling the performance of the previous generation. We unfortunately didn’t have access to a 9575F for a closer gen-on-gen comparison, but the EPYC 9755 is a decent proxy, with its 128 cores and relatively high 4.1 GHz boost clock. Compared with that CPU, the EPYC 9686F in a 2P configuration is 19% ahead — not too shabby considering the thread disadvantage.

Critically for AMD, the EPYC 9686F is also 17% ahead of Nvidia’s Vera CPU. Vera punches far above its weight class compared to the x86 competition, as evidenced by its matchup against the 9755 and Intel’s flagship Granite Rapids-AP 6980P.

Flipping over to float performance, AMD did a lot of work on the vector engine in Zen 6, and it shows up in fprate. The differences between the EPYC 9996 and 9686F are smaller, though not in a way where both chips are hamstrung. Rather, the EPYC 9696F is able to push up closer to the 9996, leaving both Zen 6 chips running circles around the field.

AMD more than doubled the score with its Zen 6 chips compared to our Zen 5 touchpoints, which is a massive achievement. The EPYC 9686F excels even more over Vera in fprate with a 40% lead, despite only a minor bump in core count (88 on Vera to 96 on the 9686F). Intel’s Granite Rapids-AP isn’t in the conversation here for raw performance; though, apparently the range is still in high-enough demand that Intel has increased production in the tight CPU environment we’re currently experiencing.

These throughput benchmarks are most representative of how these CPUs will actually be deployed, running multiple copies of applications under high concurrency. However, SPEC CPU also includes its speed suite, which looks at application performance when you can dedicate the whole chip to running just that one application, more along the lines of a client CPU benchmark.

The EPYC 9686F naturally looks better here with its blistering 5 GHz boost clock. In the integer suite, the frequency-optimized chip is 21% ahead of Vera, both in a 2P configuration. The 9996 still holds up, only falling short of the 9686F, despite much more inter-CCD communication.

SPEC shows the various improvements in Zen 6 showing up in a compounding fashion. There’s the new microarchitecture with an optimized vector path, but also more cores per CCD. And also support for higher memory speeds, as well as more memory channels. In isolation, any of these improvements are solid (if rote) for typical gen-on-gen improvements. Add them up in a workload that can take advantage of all of those improvements, however, and Zen 6 is blistering.

Two of the biggest performance vectors in the agentic era are memory performance and Python performance, at least at the moment. Agentic frameworks are largely defined by Python between LangGraph and CrewAI, though alternative frameworks written in TypeScript and Rust have started to appear as well. Regardless, Python remains important for agentic CPUs right now.

Similarly, memory bandwidth is important for certain types of agents, particularly those that need to query large databases, regardless of if they’re a traditional relational database written in SQL or a non-relational database.

Starting with the industry-standard STREAM benchmark, AMD is ahead of Nvidia’s Vera by between 11% and 19%, depending on the test. However, this was with the OpenMP “true” policy for process binding, which binds processes to the thread they’re placed on to avoid them jumping around and skewing the results. Vera performs well, even with this policy, but its mesh cache and monolithic design allow it to realize big performance gains with the “spread” policy.

This policy also binds processes, but it distributes them across the chip as far away from each other as possible. AMD chips don’t see an improvement with the spread policy, and Intel’s Granite Rapids-AP sees a slight regression. Vera, however, remains on top of the pile, with a clear victory margin with the policy applied.

In the tried-and-true MBW benchmark, the pair of 9996s claim a small lead over Vera, with a small array size of just 128 MB, but Vera jumps into first place when the array size increases. AMD has certainly tightened up the memory matchup against Vera with 16 channels and support for 12,800 MT/s MRDIMMs, but Vera’s topology and already competitive memory subsystem still wins the day.

The clear differences in memory performance flatten off as we flip to Python, which is a much more hotly-contested area of performance. Starting with a broad overview, we have PyBench. Across the suite, the EPYC 9686F was 32% faster than Vera, while the EPYC 9996 naturally claims a smaller 14% lead with its relatively limited boost clocks. For gen-on-gen comparisons, the EPYC 9996 is an impressive 24% ahead of the EPYC, while the 9686F claims a lead of 35% over the 9755 – though, no small part of that advantage is the 900 MHz higher boost clock that the 9686F has access to.

PyPerformance allows us to go more granular into specific Python applications and functions, but a similar pattern appears. The rankings of other CPUs change, but across the vast majority of tests, the EPYC 9686F leads by a clear margin. It makes sense, too, given that Python heavily leans on a single thread at stock.

There are some exceptions, notably the networkx benchmark, where the throughput-optimized 9996 tops the charts, matching Nvidia’s Vera. Despite how impressive Nvidia’s chip is, considering the company’s infancy in data center CPUs and the chip’s tame spec sheet, Venice shows that the CPU game is AMD’s to win (or lose).

Breaking out of the agentic bubble, AMD has continually proven itself as the leading force in general-purpose data center applications, and that trend continues with Venice. As with all of the workloads here, they can touch on agentic AI depending on what the agent is set up to do; database performance, for example, is important if an agent is often querying a database. However, we’re going to keep our analysis at a high level here, as EPYC CPUs will be deployed for a broad range of workloads, some portion of which won’t come remotely close to agentic AI.

Starting with compression performance, AMD generally sits at the top of the charts for compression and decompression speeds, which is the case here with zstd and 7-Zip. In zstd, Vera claims an early lead at the lowest compression level. However, its poor decompression performance shows up even at level 3, and AMD takes over with a clear victory at the compression level 19, with Vera falling even further behind in decompression performance.

The long mode results are especially interesting. Although the rankings don’t change, Vera’s performance in these sustained compression workloads gets worse, while AMD is able to hang onto much more of its performance. Vera and the EPCY 9686F are within noise of each other in the level 3 compression test, and Vera falls to the bottom of the rankings when looking at decompression.

7-Zip has been, for years now, AMD’s game. The charts really speak for themselves here; we’re dealing in multiplicative factors compared to Vera, not to mention the weak competition from Intel’s Granite Rapids-AP. AMD is always strong in 7-Zip, which is compounding here considering the already high base level of performance we see out of Zen 6 across a wide range of workloads.

Onto rendering, Blender shows big gen-on-gen gains for Venice, with the EPYC 9996 offering somewhere between a 30% and 40% improvement over the EPYC 9965 depending on the scene. This is true even in demanding scenes like Barbershop, where render time can be expressed as minutes instead of seconds for many chips. The EPYC 9996 completed the render 35% faster than the EPYC 9965. The EPYC 9686F was able to match the dense EPYC 9965, despite packing half of the cores. Vera isn’t included in our Blender results because a native Arm version isn’t available.

Vera retains a top position in C-Ray, both at 4K and 1080p, though a dual EPYC 9996 configuration is within spitting distance. The script flips in OSPRay, with AMD pulling off clear leads in real-time ambient occlusion, scientific visualization, and path tracing. There are some massive jumps, with the EPYC 9996 nearly tripling the performance of the Xeon 6980P and Vera in the ambient occlusion and scientific visualization tests.

The reworked vector engine in Venice shows up even more clearly in the dim_512 tests, which further stress both the vector engine and memory bandwidth of the platform.

We run three Java suites to measure the ever-important programming language that shows up everywhere from scientific computing to database workloads. Starting on the former end, Java SciMark shows the EPYC 9686F in a clear lead over the competition, with a massive 92% lead over Vera in the composite score. For an apples-to-apples gen-on-gen increase, the EPYC 9996 managed a respectable 24% jump over the EPYC 9965.

Java SciMark provides a nice overview of some critical functions, such as Monte Carlo risk analysis that’s applicable to financial institutions. DaCapo and Renaissance are much wider suites measuring a range of Java-based applications. Many of these tests are highly specific, so the rankings jump around, depending on what the workload stresses. We can see a bit of that with Java SciMark, as well, with Vera claiming the top slot in FFTs. Venice, however, remains ahead.

In large column-based database workloads like Clickhouse, Venice also looks impressive, though not from the jump. Vera maintains its performance from a cold cache, while none of the x86 competition reach steady performance until the cache is warm. The reason should be obvious given Vera’s topology and its distributed mesh, which allows it to jump straight to its peak performance, even when the cache is cold.

Once it’s warm, the 9686F jumps into the lead. There’s a regression in performance going from the 9965 to the 9996, which makes sense for Clickhouse, as larger core counts can create issues in cache coherency.

To look at NoSQL performance, we have simdjson, which leverages SIMD instructions for JSON parsing, as the name implies. Once again, the vector engine in Venice shines, regardless of the workload you look at.

Vera showed strong compilation performance against AMD’s Turin chips, but Team Red leapfrogs that performance with Venice. Starting with LLVM, the frequency-optimized 9686F earns a top slot with makefiles, while the EPYC 9996 tops with Ninja. The drop is around 19% gen-on-gen, aligning with the performance we’ve seen with other workloads.

In PHP, where Vera is more competitive, the EPYC 9686F squeezes out the most marginal of victories, but perhaps most telling is the 9996, which is in the upper tier of chips, despite how dense it is. Building applications, the Godot game engine and ImageMagick once again show both Venice chips on the top of the stack, outclassing the performance of Vera, which previously held a top slot in ImageMagick, and only fell slightly behind the 9755 in Godot.

The 9686F shows large performance jumps in HPC workloads, which AMD says is the target of these lower-core, frequency-optimized SKUs. In CloverLeaf, the EPYC 9686F is more than twice as fast as Vera, and it’s nearly twice as fast with FFTE. In Octave, Vera retains its top position, but both Venice chips are only slightly behind.

Although not traditional HPC, the heavily threaded number crunching behind the scenes in the Stockfish chess engine allows the 1,024 threads in a 2P EPYC 9996 server to outclass the 9965 by nearly 50%. Primesieve shows similarly large leaps, with the EPYC 9996 calculating some 37 billion prime numbers in less than half a second.

AMD already had a strong position in the newly reinvigorated CPU market with Turin. The unique topology and strong focus of Vera has allowed Nvidia to make inroads in certain workloads, but even looking at two SKUs from AMD’s broad Venice lineup, it’s clear that AMD is able to push performance much higher across the board. In some cases, that’s by orders of magnitude gen-on-gen.

There isn’t one area of Zen 6, or the SoCs available with Venice broadly, that totally accounts for the performance increase. It’s small advantages throughout the design, pushing an already strong vector engine further, vastly increasing CCD density, and giving memory bandwidth a boon between support for 12,800 MT/s MRDIMMs and 16-channel memory courtesy of the dual IODs.

But the story AMD has been trying to tell, and the one that’s difficult to ignore in the current market of a one-size-fits-all approach we’ve seen from Nvidia and Arm, is that Venice is available in a broad range of different configurations. We tested two chips on SP7, but the enterprise-focused SP8 socket is arriving within the next few months. And next year, AMD is pushing out Venice-X with 3D V-Cache for HPC workloads, as well as Verano, which looks like AMD’s take on Vera with its field-replaceable SOCAMM2 LPDDR5X memory.

Even without those chips, the two SP7 chips we tested already give AMD a leading position among current data center CPUs. Intel’s Diamond Rapids could change that story come next year, but the EPYC 9686F provides top-tier performance with HPC and AI host node performance, while the EPYC 9996 provides unmatched throughput.

Jake Roach is the Senior CPU Analyst at Tom’s Hardware, writing reviews, news, and features about the latest consumer and workstation processors.

Is ArtStation's new AI art policy good enough?

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Sign up to Creative Bloq's daily newsletter, which brings you the latest news and inspiration from the worlds of art, design and technology.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

It's trying to strike a difficult balancing act.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

When KitBash bought ArtStation, it pledged the address users concerns about the flood of AI content on the digital art platform. It took the first step back in September, when it turned on 'NoAI' for uploads by default, marking them as unavailable for AI training.

Now a new update introduces an attempt to police generative AI content along with more complete AI declarations and more control over filtering. The aim is to make it easier to users to control what they see, but it's got a mixed response from artists so far.

We're introducing the next phase of generative AI content controls. Creators will have clearer AI declaration requirements when publishing, and AI filtering is now on by default, with options that give you finer control. Learn what's changing → https://t.co/cuHmcoAuhw https://t.co/64PevA0XjmNovember 9, 2026

When publishing content on ArtStation, users will now be asked to declare whether generative AI was used in the creation of the piece. Creators can identify their work as primarily generative AI, mixed generative AI and non-generative methods or 100% non-generative.

This relies on users being upfront, and ArtStation says that most are. However, it's recognised that there is a problem with people uploading AI-generated content without flagging it as such.

That leads to the second aspect of the latest update, which is a dedicated channel for reporting AI content. If users come across artwork they think is AI-generated but which isn't labelled, they can report it. ArtStation says that every report will help improve its AI filtering for the whole community.

Users also get more control over what they're shown: they can choose whether to hide declared generative AI content, or to allow mixed generative and non-generative workflows, and they can adjust how aggressively ArtStation filters content based on user reports of suspected generative AI.

The 'Strict' setting hides artwork when just a few reports suggest it may be AI-generated. 'Balanced' filters it out when there’s a moderate level of reports and 'Loose' removes it only when user signals are strongest. By default, the filter will be set to not show any project declared as AI-generated, tagged with 'CreatedwithAI' or tagged with generative AI tools, and suspected as AI will be set to strict.

Sign up to Creative Bloq's daily newsletter, which brings you the latest news and inspiration from the worlds of art, design and technology.

The platform risks becoming useless if it's impossible to find interesting work because of the sheer volume of low-quality AI-generated content. Many users have welcomed the latest update as a move in the right direction, while others have raised doubts about how effective the measures it will be.

There's a strong reliance on user moderation, which will depend on users accurately identifying AI art. Some may still go undetected completely, while the 'Strict' filtering could remove work falsely identified as AI.

It's not yet clear if artists will be notified if their work is flagged or whether they will have a way to contest that decision. That could be a concern when even long established digital artists like Loish have been falsely accused of using AI over on platforms like TikTok.

The update highlights the difficult balancing act of policing AI content. ArtStation has a big focus on concept art, and some in the industry now see generative AI as an inevitable part of the pipeline in this field. The new Disney deal with Adobe will focus on developing custom AI models specifically for concept work. In that context, KitBash probably sees banning AI art from the platform as counterproductive if not impossible.

Exterminauts Preview: A Second Shift On Planet Tian Proves The Loop Works

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

My second shift on planet Tian came with a new account and no shortcuts. Exterminauts’ loop still delivered, solo or with friends at your back.

The last time I played Exterminauts, I was setting my editor-in-chief and one of the game’s developers on fire at gamescom. That was a curated session alongside people who knew exactly what they were doing, and it was glorious chaos from start to finish. This time, I started the way most players will when the game launches: on a brand-new account, with nothing unlocked and nobody from the studio at my shoulder.

Weaponized power tools, alien pest control, and the most friendly fire I've committed in a single session. Exterminauts is already feeling special.

Full disclosure: the Exterminauts team very kindly gave me beta access and a key, and answered some of our questions, too. I’ve played around six hours, which isn’t nearly as much as I would have liked, so I haven’t seen the deep end of the build system or the toughest contracts. What I did get was a proper look at the first few days on the job, and those days were very good.

The biggest difference for me from gamescom is the tutorial, and it’s a genuinely good introduction that gives new players a proper footing before planet Tian starts throwing things at them. From there, the game is fully committed to the bit. You start and end each mission at the same point, so every run feels like a shift: clock in, head out, deal with whatever the planet has in store, and come back to get paid. That paycheque goes back into your work gear and sponsorships.

Promotions follow the same logic. Each rank asks something different of you, from simply completing two jobs early on to finishing three of the extra tasks that pop up mid-job, or unlocking a new specification. Every promotion brings more dangerous work with it, and you can feel yourself genuinely getting better at your job. You’re not a chosen one, you’re a field worker who earned the next rung on the ladder.

If anything, the game sometimes leans a little too hard into that fantasy. At times, the whole thing has a very “we’re the lads, and we’re getting the job done” energy, that sometimes has the opposite effect of taking me out of the setting because it feels forced. Still, it does feel like Exterminauts knows very specifically who it’s trying to win over.

The jobs themselves have some nice touches. Every so often, you’ll need to weld something back together, and the game rates your accuracy while gently guiding your aim. Other tasks, like hauling a fuel tank while a teammate waits at a terminal to relaunch a device, are designed to split the work. In practice, nobody stands still at a computer while their friend gets swarmed, so it mostly turns into everyone protecting everyone.

Our preview of Turok: Origins explores the class structures, the multiplayer dynamics, and the feeling of Saber Interactive's upcoming 2026 title.

The extra jobs that pop up mid-run are the best of the bunch, because they stop you sprinting from objective to objective and pull you into corners of the map you’d otherwise never see. Some are modest, like having to kill some monsters after activating a mysterious looking device, to get rewarded with additional max health for the run. Others are much bigger, like stumbling across an abandoned mech that only one person can pilot until its ammo runs dry. It’s a lovely way of rewarding curiosity.

From there, the game is fully committed to the bit. You start and end each mission at the same point, so every run feels like a shift: clock in, head out, deal with whatever the planet has in store, and come back to get paid.

All that wandering means meeting a lot of wildlife, and the enemy variety stood out to me immediately. My favourite is the Hot Potato, a little fire monster that attacks you by rolling into you, and I can’t explain why, but the name just does it for me. It’s in wonderfully strange company, from pests that are mostly mouth and launch themselves at you, to long-necked ones firing lasers from a single giant eye, to floating balloon-like creatures that release swarms of flyers to shock you. And the planet they all call home is still a big wow, with a colour palette so dynamic that even rolling fire potatoes look lovely.

My primary weapon was, well, just a gun. What I’ll remember is the reloading: the magazines are small, which makes sense when you’ve only just started the job, but when a tide of pests rolls in and you just want to clear it and move on, constant reloads get frustrating fast. Your weapons will get stronger, but so will the enemies, and I do wonder whether they’ll still feel a little spongy for the size of your magazine.

The special weapons are a different story. I mostly used the plasma sludge launcher, which coats enemies in corrosive gunk that keeps eating away at them, and it’s brilliant against groups and huge enemies alike – though for the really big ones, grenades usually did the job faster. The Overdrive boost makes your special fire quicker and hit harder at the cost of some battery power, and watching an entire pack melt, ringleader included, is one of the best feelings in the game. I didn’t get to try it, but the CrossCut fires comically oversized saw blades that latch onto enemies and keep spinning, and charging a shot keeps them going for longer.

A review of Aliens: Fireteam Elite 2 and why it doesn't match up to the original.

Those weapons come from sponsorships, which work a little like classes, each with its own passive. I went with Bullet Crown, which boosts critical hit damage and gives you a rapid-fire SMG, because mostly playing solo, Vardhan’s shared healing didn’t make much sense. I’ll admit I was a little sad about it, though. The Vardhan gear is pink, and I would have looked very cute.

Solo play didn’t feel worse at all, which I didn’t expect. The difficulty seems to scale down appropriately when you’re alone, and it encourages a slower, more methodical style, so I spent a lot of time scoping out high ground and picking enemies off before they got close. Even on missions where perching up high wasn’t an option, it was still a blast. Difficulty runs across eight levels, adding tougher pests, extra repairs and hazards like extreme weather as you climb, but as a new hire, I didn’t get anywhere near the top.

And the planet they all call home is still a big wow, with a colour palette so dynamic that even rolling fire potatoes look lovely.

My other gripe is the extraction phase, which takes so long. Before you can leave, you have to hold off enemies while earning rewards based on your performance against your own party. Solo, when you just want a few quick runs, it drags, even with support items like sentry turrets and a device that gives you a short burst of unlimited ammo. In a group, though, being scored against your teammates is practically an invitation to friendly fire, and having already incinerated a developer, I have no qualms about doing it again.

That’s really where Exterminauts shines. I share the developers’ view that it’s best with a small group of friends, all yelling at each other while the battlefield falls apart around you. The chaos I loved at gamescom is still there; it just needs a crew to get going.

Six hours in, there’s still a real sense of discovery. The builds already look varied, the pests keep surprising me, and the loop keeps pulling me back for one more shift. There’s plenty to work towards, too, from gear upgrades and trinkets with extra passives to a season pass you can either grind through or pay to unlock. If you enjoy the tutorial and the core loop, I think the rest – the feel, the controls, that gorgeous planet – will win you over.

The bigger challenge is everything around it. There are a lot of games like this out there, and Exterminauts will have to work hard to pull people away from their current favourite. At gamescom, the team outlined six-week seasons with no progression resets, and that cadence will need to give players a reason to stay.

For now, the job is a good one. Whether it turns into a career is up to the seasons that follow.

If you and your friends are looking for deep and expansive experiences, check out these ten co-op games designed to get lost in a huge world.

Were there 'lost cosmonauts' in the early Soviet space program?

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

At the height of the Cold War, two amateur radio operators alleged they intercepted signals from Soviet cosmonauts who perished during missions that remained secret.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

In the final weeks of 1960, two young radio enthusiasts were monitoring transmissions from a home-built listening station on a rooftop in Turin, Italy, when the brothers detected something extraordinary: a faint signal that bore the hallmarks of one originating from space. That wasn't the only surprising thing about the alleged signal:, the transmission carried a very familiar message: it was Morse code for the international distress signal SOS.

As the youths listened, the signal seemed to grow weaker. This raised a troubling possibility: could it be that the signal's source was moving farther away from Earth? And if so, was it possible that this concerning signal was of Soviet origin — perhaps a spacecraft drifting helplessly into space, carrying a cosmonaut on board whose doomed mission had never been publicly acknowledged?

These strange events from November 1960 became the first in a series of alleged radio signal interceptions that would form the basis of an enduring Cold War-era mystery involving so-called "lost cosmonauts" — early Soviet space travelers rumored to have died during secret spaceflight missions before Yuri Gagarin's historic flight the following spring, in April 1961.Today, more than six decades later, the story remains one of the most haunting legends of the Space Age. Were there truly early Soviet space missions — with tragically ill-fated cosmonaut crews — that remain undisclosed to this day?

The peculiar story began in October 1957, as the Italian brothers Achille and Gian Battista Judica-Cordiglia learned, along with the rest of the world, about the Soviet Union's successful launch of Sputnik 1, Earth's first artificial satellite.

Immediately, the two became fascinated with the idea of trying to listen in on signals from such spacecraft and began working to assemble their very own radio tracking station, which they put together mostly from secondhand military surplus equipment. What they couldn't easily find and purchase, the brothers would often assemble themselves. They built an improvised tracking station in an abandoned World War Two bunker, which the brothers dubbed Torre Bert, that eventually became a fully functional radio facility with an antenna they built from salvaged materials like chicken wire and scraps of metal.

Whatever it lacked in aesthetics, the station made up for in functionality, and soon the brothers' amateur efforts reportedly allowed them to successfully intercept transmissions associated with Sputnik 2, the first Soviet spacecraft to carry a living creature into orbit: a dog named Laika, who became a one-way passenger on the mission. Later, the boys also said they were able to track transmissions from America's Explorer 1 satellite.

Some of what the Judica-Cordiglia brothers were able to achieve could be chalked up to being in the right place at the right time. Specifically, their location in northern Italy was advantageous, since it allowed them to monitor signals from Soviet spacecraft passing overhead.

By late November 1960, the Judica-Cordiglia brothers learned of reports from a West German observatory suggesting there may have been an unidentified satellite in orbit, raising an intriguing possibility: could there have been a spacecraft launch that had not been disclosed to the public?

Intrigued, the brothers began spending their evenings at Torre Bert scanning the skies for radio frequencies in search of the alleged mystery object. Eventually, they claimed to have detected a signal that differed significantly from those they had tracked in the past. Unlike the familiar electronic beeps they had intercepted from other satellites, this transmission seemed unmistakable: it was Morse code for SOS.

According to their account, as the pair listened to the alarming transmission, the signal gradually began to fade, raising a disconcerting possibility: what if the spacecraft they were monitoring hadn't merely been traveling through orbit, but had been drifting away from Earth?

If correct, the implications were staggering: the Soviet Union may have launched a secret space mission, but this time with a human cosmonaut on board — and something had gone terribly wrong, ending with a spacecraft and its occupant becoming lost in space.

Significantly, the brothers appear to have reached this conclusion independently, and no verified evidence has ever surfaced establishing that the transmission they claimed to have intercepted originated from a crewed spacecraft, let alone one associated with an unacknowledged Soviet mission that somehow escaped Earth's gravitational influence, with tragic results.

Nonetheless, the brothers' claims would soon help give rise to a disturbing possibility: that there had been unknown "lost" cosmonauts who perished during missions that remained hidden from public knowledge.

The alleged distress signal was only the beginning of a series of unsubstantiated claims the Judica-Cordiglia brothers made, and by the first days of February 1961, the amateur radio sleuths would claim to have intercepted another odd signal. This time, the pair claimed to have heard labored breathing coming across the airwaves, and even what resembled the strained moans of a person aboard an orbiting spacecraft.

In an equally eerie recording the brothers produced, what sounded like a rapidly beating human heart could be heard. The brothers shared the recording with Italian cardiologist Achille Dogliotti, whose examination reportedly led him to conclude that the sounds were consistent with strained breathing and an elevated human heartbeat.

With time, more recordings would begin to appear, featuring transmissions the boys believed to be evidence of unsuccessful Soviet missions that preceded the successful spaceflight of Yuri Gagarin, whose 108-minute journey around Earth in 1961 made him the first human to travel into space.

However, what may be the brothers' most controversial claim involved a recording they believed captured the final moments of a female cosmonaut whose ill-fated journey allegedly occurred prior to the historic 1963 spaceflight of Valentina Tereshkova, the first woman to travel into space. However, experts have pointed out that these transmissions do not follow typical military communications protocols, further casting doubt on their legitimacy.

As the Judica-Cordiglia brothers' notoriety increased, journalists and curiosity-seekers weren't the only ones whose attention they reportedly began to attract; their activities also allegedly garnered interest from intelligence officials.

In one instance, the brothers claimed a man identifying himself as a Russian reporter had visited them. Shortly thereafter, another individual — this time claiming to be an Italian counterintelligence agent — warned them that the "journalist" who had come to see them was a KGB agent.

The Judica-Cordiglias and their unconventional hobby, it seemed, had now placed them in the crosshairs of Soviet intelligence officials.

An unusual twist in this part of the Italian brothers' story would eventually emerge decades later, when London-based journalist Kris Hollington began reinvestigating their claims for a feature story that appeared in the paranormal-focused magazine Fortean Times. According to Hollington, a former Soviet KGB operative who later served as a Russian ambassador to a European country had reportedly confirmed (on condition of anonymity) that Moscow was indeed interested in the Judica-Cordiglias and their claims.

"Our cosmonauts were warned to be careful what they said while in space after this and we had the brothers followed," the former KGB agent reportedly told Hollington.

Things could have ended tragically for the brothers if it were not for the fame they achieved at the time for their alleged radio interceptions. As the former KGB agent told Hollington, it was believed for a time that the brothers "had to be dealt with — an accident perhaps," although after they appeared on a television program that brought them significant attention, any Soviet plans that would have endangered the brothers became impractical.

"That saved their lives," the former KGB agent reportedly told Hollington, who expressed gratitude for the circumstances that may well have saved the Italian brothers, with the former agent confiding to Hollington that he thought "they were good kids."

Of course, the claims of alleged Soviet surveillance are difficult to verify given the anonymity requested by Hollington's source. The same must be said of many of the claims made by the Judica-Cordiglias — even if the brothers were successful in intercepting Soviet transmissions at the time, that alone does not prove that they uncovered evidence of unacknowledged missions during which cosmonauts may have perished.

Some, like spaceflight researcher Sven Grahn, have acknowledged the genuine tracking capabilities the brothers possessed while remaining skeptical of the more sensational "lost cosmonaut" claims, suggesting the brothers might have misinterpreted some of the transmissions they recorded.

"I think that the Judica-Cordiglia brothers did run a tracking station and picked up signals from various spacecraft," Grahn said in an assessment of the brothers and their claims. "However, for some reason they thought that they needed sensational stories [to] maintain their image of a 'hot-shot' operation," he adds. "Once they over-interpreted some receptions and made fantastic claims they were 'trapped' and had to continue to produce sensations."

There is also the possibility that the recordings were hoaxes, of course. Whatever the case, there are no independently verified launch records, authenticated communications, or other evidence from the period that provides additional support for what the brothers claimed to have intercepted.

Something that can be verified, however, is the Soviet Union's well-documented secrecy, adding a further layer of complication to matters. As space historian James Oberg has observed, there are verified instances in which Soviet officials altered photographs and records from the early years of human spaceflight, a fact that has perhaps helped fuel rumors about alleged missing cosmonauts. It is also known that the Soviet space program covered up several deadly disasters, such as a launch pad explosion on Oct. 24, 1960 that killed a high-ranking Soviet military officer and at least 100 others. At the time, Soviet state news outlets reported it as a plane crash.

No verified evidence has emerged to support claims of failed, unacknowledged Soviet crewed spaceflights of the kind described in the lost-cosmonaut legends. Yet the Judica-Cordiglia brothers' accounts have drawn attention for decades and remain among the strangest stories in the lore of the early Space Age — a fact that illustrates humanity's enduring fascination with Cold War mysteries and phenomena that exist beyond Earth.

Micah Hanks is a science reporter and podcaster focusing on space and astronomy, and Editor at TheDebrief.org. Follow him on X @MicahHanks.

A closer look at Verano, AMD's first step toward a Zen-based superchip to rival Nvidia's Vera Rubin

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Cores, clocks, memory, and interconnects all perfectly balanced for a singular mission: keeping instinct fed.

AMD's upcoming Verano processors aren't like any Epyc CPUs we've seen before. Rather than general-purpose workloads, Verano is designed specifically to power AMD's next generation of GPU servers.

In an interview with The Register during a press event in Las Vegas last week, Madhu Rangarajan, CVP of AMD's compute and enterprise AI products business, described Verano as being the first step towards building something that more closely resembles Nvidia's Vera Rubin superchips.

"Verano is really designed for an integrated CPU+GPU rack scale solution," he said. "From that perspective, I wouldn't say the CPU is a competitor to Vera. In a head note, I'd say the Verano+GPU solution is a competitor to Vera+Rubin."

For reference, Vera Rubin and the GB200 and GB300 that came before it aren't your typical server platforms. Instead they're more like an enormous single-board computer combining CPUs and GPUs into a tightly-integrated package. Each of Nvidia's superchips joins a single 88-core Vera CPU and 1.5 TB of LPDDR5X memory to a pair of Rubin GPUs via Nvidia's speedy NVLink-C2C interconnect.

Rangarajan tells us Verano will be the host CPU for a similar class of product using AMD GPUs, and much like Nvidia's superchips, the CPU will also use notebook memory rather than conventional RDIMMS.

However, unlike Vera, which is also being deployed as a standalone CPU in storage servers and agent sandboxes, Verano has a singular purpose: keeping its GPUs fed. (We assume they'll be MI500X GPUs.)

This design choice reflects AMD's philosophy of one SKU per workload. As such, Verano's core count, clock speeds, memory bandwidth, power, and I/O have been rebalanced to keep its GPUs fed and not much else.

If we were to peel back Verano's heat spreader, the chip looks a lot like the Venice Epycs we looked at back in September. It features either six of AMD's core complex dies (CCDs) for a total of 72 high-frequency cores clocking to 5.3 GHz, or 192 density-optimized cores clocking to 4.1 GHz, depending on whether the customer's workloads prefer core density or faster clocks.

But that's about where the visual similarities end. Much of the chip has been reworked to eliminate data flow bottlenecks between the CPU and the GPUs it is responsible for feeding. The chip features an entirely custom pair of I/O dies through which four LPDDR5X memory controllers are attached.

Those memory controllers are currently being validated for use with 24 channels of memory at up to 8,533 MT/s. We're told that we may see that creep up a bit depending on how much headroom AMD has when the part starts shipping next year, but for now that's what AMD is validating against.

16 channels of DDR5, 5 GHz clock speeds, and support for CXL 3.1 memory god boxes? What's not to like?

That means the platform not only has fewer cores, but also slower memory. Peak theoretical memory bandwidth tops out at around 800 GB/s, which you might have noticed is half of what AMD's top-specced Venice parts can do and about 66 percent of Nvidia's Vera.

What LPDDR seems to buy AMD is a 30 to 40 percent reduction in power consumption compared to RDIMMS, as well as a more compact footprint for liquid cooling. But it also comes with some downsides. In its first incarnation, Verano won't be socketed. Instead it'll use a ball-grid array (BGA) that's soldered to the board, which will make it harder to service.

"LPDDR tends to be quite finicky in terms of speeds. Every time you go through a connector, you tend to lose a pin or two, and you already have one connector with the SoCam itself. So to minimize any of those losses in speed, we went BGA on the CPU," Rangarajan told us.

Unlike with a lot of notebooks, the memory itself won't be soldered down and will use the same SOCAMM2 modules found in Nvidia's Vera-Rubin and Vera-Vera CPU Superchips.

Even so, Verano looks like a bit of a downgrade compared to Venice except when it comes to CPU-to-GPU connectivity.

Historically, AMD's CPU-to-GPU communication has been done over PCIe. It works but has usually required PCIe switches to keep up with all the GPUs, NICs, and storage on board. Nvidia's superchip dispensed with this, replacing PCIe with its NVLink-C2C interconnect, which provides 900 GB/s of bidirectional bandwidth to each of the system's GPUs.

Venice and Verano do something similar. Unlike past Instinct accelerators, AMD's latest generation of GPUs aren't limited to PCIe for CPU-to-GPU communication. Instead, starting with Venice, AMD transitioned to using xGMI.

Global memory interconnect (GMI) is how AMD has moved data between chiplets on its CPUs, GPUs, and APUs for several generations now. xGMI extends this to GPU-to-GPU and CPU-to-GPU communications.

With Venice, xGMI link bandwidth tops out at 64 Gbps per lane, which the keen-eyed among you have probably already figured out is the same as PCIe 6.0. Verano takes this to another level. As we mentioned earlier, Verano disaggregated the memory controllers, and AMD appears to have reclaimed that space for fatter serializer deserializers (SerDes). Instead of 64 Gbps per lane, Verano's xGMI links top out at 112 Gbps, effectively giving it near PCIe 7.0 levels of bandwidth years before the spec ships in production CPUs.

Rangarajan explains this is driven by what AMD's GPUs need to run optimally.

AMD hasn't disclosed the ratio of CPUs to GPUs it's aiming for with Verano, so it's difficult to say how it compares to Nvidia. In a 2:1 ratio of GPUs to CPUs like we saw in Vera Rubin, Verano would deliver identical chip-to-chip bandwidth, while in a 1:4 ratio like we see in AMD's Helios racks, we'd be looking at about half the bandwidth at 448 GB/s. It's also not clear whether AMD has increased the number of xGMI links per I/O die for Verano or not, so bandwidth per GPU could be even higher. We expect more info closer to the MI500X's debut next year.

AMD doesn't expect Verano to replace Venice in every AI rack: The chip is designed specifically for use with Instinct accelerators.  Moreover, AMD isn't waiting for Verano to roll out its Helios rackscale systems. As we discussed during Advancing AI earlier this year, those systems will feature 18 96-core Venice CPUs paired to 72 Instinct MI455X GPUs.

Moving forward, AMD expects its Venice to remain a popular host processor for AI head nodes not just for its own GPUs but for the growing number of homegrown accelerators built by the great model houses. ®

Bill Hader Has Highbrow Taste in Children’s Movies

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

You can trust Bill Hader on movies. From gushing about John Ford and Akira Kurosawa in the Criterion Closet, to spoofing Errol Morris and D.A. Pennebaker in Documentary Now!, to creating, directing and starring in four seasons of HBO’s Barry—the SNL alum’s cinematic expertise is well-documented. Hader knows a thing or two about kids’ movies in particular, having lent his iconic voice to animated tentpoles like Inside Out and Cloudy With a Chance of Meatballs. This week, you can hear Hader in The Cat in The Hat, a new adaptation of the 1957 Dr. Seuss classic, in which he voices the titular troublemaking feline.

Written and directed by Alessandro Carloni and Erica Rivinoja, this new iteration of the beloved children’s story is an inviting romp that is mischievous enough for kids, grounded enough for grown-ups, and teeming with gags and good nature that all can enjoy. The film is an ideal showcase for Hader’s gymnastic vocal talents, and also boasts Quinta Brunson, Matt Berry, and Giancarlo Esposito among its cast. (It’s a more family-friendly—though no less amusing—take on the Cat than when Hader last played the character, in a 2014 SNL sketch.)

A father to three daughters, Hader has seen it all—from Frozen and Tangled to Off Campus—and does his part, when possible, to pass down the classic films that were formative during his own childhood. “You don’t want to push it too hard,” he tells GQ on a recent call. “For every one I show them, there’s 10 that I start and they go, ‘Ugh, Dad, this is boring.’” The rest of us, however, are eager to take note. Below, Hader shares some of his favorite quote-unquote “kids’ movies” whose cinematic merit makes them a must-see at any age.

“When I was young, my dad told me that was a movie that he watched as a kid. I didn't grow up in a time when there were a lot of Westerns, but [for] my dad and uncles, that was all that was on TV.

What I was moved by was the relationship [between] Alan Ladd as Shane and this little boy [Brandon deWilde] enamored with a gunfighter. It has this heroic sensibility to it, but underneath was this honesty of, ‘You don't want that life,’ which I thought was interesting. To me, Van Heflin, the dad, was the cooler guy. So it upended those expectations of what the movie was going to be.

It has this ending where it's poignant and kind of sad, but I remember thinking, ‘Oh, that's good that that's what happens at the end of the story.’ It's a good coming-of-age story… It's kind of sneaky. You could see it on the face of it just being this cut-and-dry, Western-hero type of movie. But when you really think about it, even as a kid, I got a sense that there's a lot of depth to it.”

“Buster Keaton is one of my favorites, and that was one I showed my kids. I have three daughters. Now they're 17, 14, and 12, but at the time, they were very little. I'm a movie nerd, so I was gonna show them some silent movies, and I was like, ‘I know. Everybody's gonna roll their eyes at me for showing the kids silent movies. How pretentious.’ But they really responded to Buster Keaton, specifically Sherlock Jr. and Three Ages, his take on the movie Intolerance by D.W. Griffith. They just loved it, and it was fun getting to watch it through their eyes.

I recently took my youngest [to see] it at the Aero Theater in LA, and it still was great. To see a movie that was made over a hundred years ago still work on all [levels], like… it works better than most movies now. It's just very simple. [Keaton] wasn't some kind of auteur guy. He was a vaudeville comedian that was curious about filmmaking; what you could do with editing, what you could do with shots. And I think he ended up making the blueprint of how you shoot physical comedy and action. It’s pretty amazing.

What's interesting about some of those silent movies is that they don't really know what they're doing. The best stuff comes out of, ‘I don't know, I had this idea, let's figure it out in a really open, curious way.’ And in doing that, you invent the language.

Charlie Chaplin, too. [I’ve shown] them City Lights and The Circus and The Gold Rush. But they were really drawn to Buster Keaton, I think, because he was kind of dry and you didn't really know what he was thinking, and so you could put all your feelings onto him.”

“That's one that my parents showed me when I was a kid. It's a long movie, but it has so many crazy [moments] and a lot of physical comedy. I just love all those comedians in the movie. Every time someone pulls up, you're like, ‘Oh my God, it's Jonathan Winters. I love Phil Silvers. Oh my God, Spencer Tracy's in this movie!’ Dick Shawn is my personal favorite in that movie, the guy who's the real mama's boy.

It's kind of the one Stanley Kramer movie I like. Stanley Kramer movies tended to be, I don't know, a little sentimental, or overtly didactic or something, [but] this movie is just having a ton of fun. I have very fond memories of watching it with my parents and my grandparents. My dad was in town recently, and when I came home, he was watching it with my kids, and they were all laughing really hard and telling me scenes from it.

They really loved it, but [just like] with the silent movies, you don't want to push it too hard. For every [classic movie] I show them, there's 10 that I start and they go, ‘Ugh, Dad, this is boring,’ and I go, ‘Alright, we can watch Nobody Wants This or Off Campus.’”

“The Secret of NIMH was one that I loved as a kid, because it was ostensibly a kid's movie, but it was incredibly dark and disturbing. It has stuff that I still find really frightening. And I loved the mythology of it. As a kid, I liked the idea that in your backyard [there] could be this whole kingdom that you don't even know about.

There's specifically a shot in it with the crow, played by Dom DeLuise, and in the distance they see this cat. There's this long shot [of] this field, and then suddenly, just really quickly, you see this cat pop up and go back down under [as] it's trying to catch a bird or something. That shot had a huge effect on me. I thought, ‘Oh man, that's so terrifying.’

And then the story of the rats and NIMH, and they're all being tested on, and they fall into this abyss, and the whole ending where they're having this sword fight… I just thought it was fantastic. Everything was high stakes. And it wasn't Disney. I liked Disney, but Disney never went that dark.

Maybe not a lot of people agree with me, [but] I’m definitely someone [that] likes when something's scary for a kid, within reason. A lot of people are like, ‘No, no, no, they can't see that.’ And it's like, ‘No, let them see it. Get scared. That's good.’ I was happy that I was scared.

I'm sure if The Secret of NIMH came out now, they would have a whole room of executives going, ‘You can't have the owl with its head upside-down, turning around and with cobwebs all over him. It's just too scary.’ But for me, it scared me to death, and then I would keep watching it, and then it became less scary, and then I started to appreciate it. I think it's good for kids to be scared.”

“That movie came out when I was a freshman in high school. I got a job on the school paper, and my first job was to write a review of all the new movies, and it was the week that that movie came out. And so I remember I went with a notebook, I was writing my thoughts down, and then I ended up writing a pretty bad [review]. I liked [the movie] a lot, but my actual writing was terrible. The editors took it and rewrote it all.

But I loved it. I love Tim Burton. I loved the music in it, specifically the song that Catherine O'Hara sings as Sally; I think it's such a moving song. And Henry Selick’s direction in it is amazing. People always think Tim Burton made that movie. He didn't. It's Henry Selick. He spent every day there with those animators making that thing work.

I love the scope of it and how grand it is, from the beginning—that ‘This Is Halloween’ song, there's something almost operatic about it. And it's its own thing. The best stuff, to me, is always when the filmmakers and the writers [are] doing a thing that they want to see, and they trust an audience will get it.

When it came out, I remember it wasn't what it is now. It was kind of this weird oddity. And then as time went on… I remember moving to LA, and it was the 10-year anniversary of it, and they had a screening someplace, and I drove past all these people dressed like the characters, they had Jack Skellington t-shirts on and all that stuff, and I went, ‘Wow, that's crazy.’ Cause I was a huge fan of it, and had the soundtrack, and I absolutely adored that movie.

[Once] I had kids, it was one of the first movies I showed them, and they watched it every Halloween and every Christmas. My youngest daughter [recently] played Sally in a school play, and she got to sing that song, and so it was full circle. I got very emotional. I was like, ‘Oh my gosh, there she is, singing that song.’ And I had become friendly with Catherine O'Hara, and she passed away, and it was really… Yeah, I was a blubbering idiot when she started singing that song.”
