‘Sabotage’: Read Hegseth’s Fiery Speech Against His Impeachment

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Politics  |  Read Source →

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

Transcript: ‘Sabotage’: Read Hegseth’s Fiery Speech Against His Impeachment

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

Get audio access with any FP subscription.

U.S. Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth held a rare press conference on Tuesday morning as he faced the prospect of impeachment, delivering an uncompromising and combative speech in defense of his record while excoriating his political opponents.

“What one disgruntled and defeated congressman is attempting is not oversight, and it’s not accountability. It’s a political spectacle, a personal manifesto driven by ego and resentment. Time for one man’s final days in Congress to try to do damage to this president; this country; and most irresponsibly, the warriors who defend it. It’s a farce, and it’s a stunt, and everybody knows it,” Hegseth said at the beginning of his remarks.

U.S. Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth held a rare press conference on Tuesday morning as he faced the prospect of impeachment, delivering an uncompromising and combative speech in defense of his record while excoriating his political opponents.

“What one disgruntled and defeated congressman is attempting is not oversight, and it’s not accountability. It’s a political spectacle, a personal manifesto driven by ego and resentment. Time for one man’s final days in Congress to try to do damage to this president; this country; and most irresponsibly, the warriors who defend it. It’s a farce, and it’s a stunt, and everybody knows it,” Hegseth said at the beginning of his remarks.

The impeachment effort, led by outgoing Republican Rep. Thomas Massie, largely focuses on the Defense Department chief’s central role in the Iran war but also pertains to other major military actions under his watch—including strikes on alleged drug trafficking boats near Latin America and the operation that led to the capture of Venezuelan President Nicolás Maduro.

Massie, who introduced eight articles of impeachment against the defense secretary on Monday, accuses Hegseth of waging war against Iran without congressional approval, abusing the powers of his office, and violating federal law and the Constitution.

The House is scheduled to hold an initial procedural vote on impeachment on Tuesday evening.

Below is the full transcript of Hegseth’s speech.—John Haltiwanger

Well, good morning. I’ll get right to it. What one disgruntled and defeated congressman is attempting is not oversight, and it’s not accountability. It’s a political spectacle, a personal manifesto driven by ego and resentment. Time for one man’s final days in Congress to try to do damage to this president; this country; and most irresponsibly, the warriors who defend it. It’s a farce, and it’s a stunt, and everybody knows it.

In the 250 years of our American republic, no secretary of war or secretary of defense has ever been impeached for carrying out lawful military operations, and in our case, successful ones at that. Not once, war powers resolution or not. Not under [former U.S. President Ronald] Reagan in Lebanon, not under [former U.S. President Bill] Clinton in Kosovo, not under [former U.S. President Barack] Obama in Iraq and Syria, not under [former U.S. President Joe] Biden in Iraq and Syria. That’s the standard. The history is clear, and it’s very straightforward. And one disgruntled congressman wants to shred it over policy disagreements. That’s what it is: policy disagreements, not high crimes, not misdemeanors, not treason, not bribery, not the kinds of offenses the founders intended when they wrote the impeachment clause.

The proper remedy for a policy disagreement—it’s legislation, it’s debate, it’s the next presidential election. It’s not handing the House and Senate floor to the Democrats so that they can run a show trial against the president and the secretary of war while Republicans still control Congress and should be delivering wins for the American people. You see, we’ve seen this show before. They impeached [U.S.] President [Donald] Trump over a perfect phone call. They impeached him again in his final week in office. Both times they failed. Both times he came back stronger, and the American people sent him back to the White House.

Now, they’re trying a third time. This time against a member of his cabinet. They will fail again, and if standing with President Trump puts me in that company, I’ll take that company every day of the week.

Let’s be clear about what this resolution actually does. It tells our enemies—Iran, the cartels, every adversary watching—that the United States will actually punish its own warriors and its own civilian leadership for successfully executing lawful military operations. It tells them that a single rogue legislator, loaded with personal animus for the president, can attempt to veto national security decisions made by the duly elected commander in chief. It puts a target on the backs of American warriors by signaling that Congress will undermine their missions in real time. This is not patriotism. This is sabotage.

You know, five years ago, America was humiliated in Kabul, Afghanistan, and every enemy on earth took notice. But somehow, as the president pointed out yesterday—no impeachment vote for [former U.S. Defense Secretary] Lloyd Austin for that terrible withdrawal. Not even one vote. Or no impeachment vote for when he went AWOL for over a week. No impeachment vote for when he let a Chinese spy balloon fly over the country for over a week. See, President Trump ended that kind of wokeness and weakness. Peace through strength, not as a slogan, but as a way of war.

The Houthis found out first. They fired on American ships. Biden had let it happen. President Trump said, “no more.” Operation Rough Rider hit them more than 1,000 times. They cried uncle, and they stopped shooting at our ships to this day. Iran found out next. Operation Midnight Hammer, B-2s from the heartland, halfway around the world, straight into Fordow, Natanz, and Esfahan—flawless. The largest and most successful B-2 strike in American history. Nuclear sites obliterated.

Later in Operation Epic Fury, by every measure, we achieved a historic military victory. Under 40 days of sustained combat, more than 13,000 targets struck. Their navy sunk, their air force grounded, their defense industrial base gone, more than 80 percent of their nuclear industrial base gone, their military in ruin, the ironclad blockade unbroken. We control the Strait of Hormuz. Crazy Islamists in Tehran will never, ever have a nuclear weapon. A historic effort, only made possible by a courageous commander in chief. History will judge the Iran war quite differently than conventional wisdom right now. We didn’t start this war; Iran did, 47 years ago.

But President Trump is willing to do what no other president would. He did the tough, right thing—not for politics, but for the safety and the security of the American people. That’s leadership.

He did not kick the can; he kicked their teeth in. For generations—my generation fought other Middle Eastern wars that dragged on for years, for decades. We lost thousands, thousands of Americans. I wear the bracelet of one of them on my left wrist every single day. It reminds me.

And may I remind you, for perspective, we lost 60,000 Americans in Vietnam over 20 years. We lost 37,000 in Korea in just three years. That many humans are lost on the front lines of Ukraine and Russia every month. This—Epic Fury—has never been that, and it never will be. The media can just never admit the military success because they, you, hate the president more than you love our country or our troops.

Then, in our own hemisphere, we started at home from day one. President Trump made homeland security national security. We sent American troops to lock down the southwest border and choked off the cartel pipeline of human smuggling, of drugs, and death. And then, we took the fight to them. The cartels thought they could just poison the American people and hide from justice. So we formed a counter-cartel coalition, designated them terrorists, and hunted them at sea, and now on land. More than 70 narco-vessels sunk; 218 narcoterrorists dead, where they should be; maritime smuggling down at least 65 percent and dropping even further. Thousands of American lives saved with every drug boat sunk and every shipment prevented.

Indicted and wanted, Nicolás Maduro thought he was untouchable. He was not kidnapped. He was brought to justice by America’s best. Victory in one night. Victory in 48 minutes, to be precise. And today, he sits in front of an American judge in New York City. Flawless success.

Panama kicked China’s Belt and Road Initiative to the curb, and we, along with them, control the canal. The message is simple: This is our hemisphere. Act accordingly.

Our NATO allies, after decades of riding on the American taxpayer in Europe, committed to 5 percent of GDP for their own defense. Our allies are finally stepping up. Out with being a burden, in with burden sharing. Why? Because President Trump demanded it. America is the only country on Earth, and this military the only one, that could successfully execute and lead all of these operations and efforts. Sheer success, victories in both Venezuela and Iran, energy security in our hemisphere, in a way none thought possible for a generation, and no nuclear bomb for Iran ever.

But of course, security doesn’t come free. Nobody was lost in the mission in Venezuela, but 18 Americans have given their lives in Operation Epic Fury. We know their names. I’ve been to Dover. We honor them every day, but their sacrifice only stiffens our resolve.

As it happens, tomorrow is Veterans Day. This year, the operators of Absolute Resolve and the warriors of Epic Fury will mark it for the first time. They stand shoulder to shoulder with the pilots of Midnight Hammer and the sailors of Rough Rider who went before them. They answered the call, they executed, they won, and on the eve of Veterans Day, these resolutions tell every one of them that their service was a crime.

Their service was not a crime. These are heroes, and no politician in Washington will tell them otherwise while I hold this office. President Trump gave this department what it has not had in a generation: a commander in chief with the courage to lead and the resolve to win. I stand behind every one of these operations, I was proud to execute them, and I would do it again tomorrow.

And while this defeated politician—defeated ironically by a Navy SEAL, I might add—while this defeated politician drafts articles of impeachment, we’ve delivered the first increase, and a big one, in hostile fire and imminent danger pay in more than 20 years. We’ve reinstated over 600 service members who were forced out under the previous administration’s COVID[-19] mandate. All five of our services broke historic recruiting records: the Army, the Navy, the Air Force, the Space Force, the Marines for two years in a row. In fact, the Marines in FY27 met half of their goal on day one. Americans are lining up to serve in President Trump’s Department of War. And by the way, to those Marines and all those Marines out there, semper fi and happy 251st birthday today.

Two years of historic recruitment after Biden couldn’t recruit anyone. Same with retention, by the way, through the roof. That means the troops that are in are staying in. The best of the best, based on merit, staying in. Night and day from Biden to Trump. We cut unnecessary senior officer billets by 20 percent and raised combat performance standards. Through the One Big Beautiful Bill, we’re investing in troop pay, in housing, in healthcare, in advanced weapons and equipment our warfighters actually need to win. That’s the record. That’s what we’re defending.

Make no mistake about what is happening. This is not about me. Politicians in Washington have accused the sailors and the pilots who struck narcoterrorist boats of war crimes for doing their job. Senators in cute videos have suggested the same. This resolution is the next step. Put the secretary on trial today, and the men and women who carried out those orders tomorrow. If they want to come for the warriors, they’re going to have to come through me.

As for the articles themselves, Iran, the cartels, Maduro, Yemen. Every one was a lawful operation ordered by the commander in chief, then ordered by myself, notified to Congress, and executed by American warriors who did their jobs. We destroyed terror regimes’ capabilities. We sank the boats carrying poison into our communities. We put a narco-dictator in handcuffs. These are not high crimes. This is the job, and it’s the job well done.

For the first time in American history, a cabinet secretary overseeing America’s military is being attacked not for losing conflicts with our enemies, but for winning them. Condemned not for defeat, but for military victory. The disgruntled congressman even admitted this himself yesterday. No failure here, just impeachment for success.

As to the claim that the American military targets civilians, well, that is a lie and that is a slander against every professional who flew, sailed, and fought on these missions. The only people who target civilians are the terrorists that we hunt, and I will dignify this claim no further. None of these are high crimes or misdemeanors; they’re partisan policy disagreements from people who want to politicize our military.

We will not apologize for putting American lives first. We will not apologize for stopping the flow of drugs that kill our citizens. We will not apologize for destroying the capabilities of a regime that chants “Death to America,” and if that regime makes one wrong move, as the president said, we remain locked and loaded with the full weight and lethality of Central Command. We will not apologize for restoring a military that recruits based on merit, restores real standards, and fights to win.

These resolutions are a farce. They mock the Constitution. They attack the chain of command, and they criminalize the warfighters who execute lawful orders. This process hands our adversaries a propaganda victory and wastes the time of Congress that should instead be focused on protecting the American people.

We will continue to do the job the American people elected President Trump to do: protect this country, defeat our enemies, and rebuild a military worthy of the warriors who wear the uniform.

I pray each day, and I hope you do too, for our troops. They are the best of our nation. Like the president, I will defend them with every fiber of my being, every single day. That includes having their back, no matter what. So to the troops, as I’ve said before: Don’t blink, ignore the noise, never hesitate. This is all politics. You do real things. Stay the course. May Almighty God protect them always, and may our actions today and every day bring glory to Him. We will stand, we will fight, and we will defend this republic against all enemies, foreign and domestic. And amen.

French judo star Teddy Riner may have announced his bid for the 2027 French presidential campaign on Monday, but he is not the only sports star to transition from stadium to bully pulpit. Read about some of his most famous predecessors.

Airport Lounges Are Packed—Travelers Demand $5,000 Annual Credit Card Fees To Keep The Masses Out

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Airport lounges are so crowded that travelers are demanding $200 admission prices and $5,000 annual credit card fees to keep the “untouchables” out, arguing that the “true lounge caste” has been driven into the concourse while everyone else crowds around the buffet. There are also lounges where the line outside and the crowd inside make the benefit nearly worthless. Paying for somewhere comfortable to sit, and then being unable to find a seat, has become a really legitimate complaint – even if it’s mixed with real snobbery because it’s the ‘other’ people who shouldn’t be in there. “Premium” travel experience before 10am on a Sunday https://t.co/13PniAuksG — David Slotnick (@David_Slotnick) November 8, 2026 Status anxiety places airline lounges belong alongside BMWs, remote work, and ski trips as things that have lost their cachet as access spread. They’re still aspirational to the people getting in, while the people already inside are unhappy that increased access has lowered their relative status. View wyatt.today’s post on Twitter The original poster’s follow-up invokes the tens of thousands of his employer’s dollars he spends on flights, then uses a racial slur for other guests. It’s no longer about finding empty space inside. But most people just want a chance to use the benefit they’re paying for and maybe a snack without standing in line in the terminal like a schmuck to get an $895 ‘free’ drink. Wanting Quiet Space Isn’t Elitist One reply argues that a lounge defeats its purpose when it gets too crowded. It may still have food, a shower, or an airline agent to help when your flight gets delayed or cancels. But the core value proposition is escape from the terminal. I often just want a place to open my laptop without balancing it on my knees. And then having frictionless access to a snack, and the ability to run into the washroom without bringing all my stuff with me, are cherries on top. Nobel laureate James Buchanan’s theory of clubs explains this well. Sharing a facility spreads its costs across more people. Eventually, adding people creates enough congestion to make the experience worse. The membership and the size of the facility have to be considered together. There need to be access limits to preserve the value proposition of the facility. But since most people don’t use the space most of the time, it’s perceived option value that they’re purchasing and it continues to drive premium credit card acquisition and adoption. Lounges Got Better, And People Started Using Them Differently I’ve written about the claim that airport lounge culture represents American decline. The nostaglic view is just wrong. I remember ginger ale and Goldfish crackers, a photocopier in the Admirals Club, and packaged Tillamook cheese in United’s Red Carpet Clubs. Today’s better lounges offer actual meals and craft cocktails. When I visited Chase’s new Dallas–Fort Worth lounge, I found the best food inside a bank lounge that isn’t a Capital One Landing. Plus they have whiskey tastings, and facial treatments. The space is 18,000 square feet. These spaces are no longer just a room with seating, snacks and a fax machine. The same improvements help explain why lounges fill up. People go for the food People who might have stopped in to check e-mail now plan to eat a meal. They come more often and stay longer. A better lounge becomes a destination in its own right. The travel mix has changed. Traditional managed business travel is a smaller part of the story. Premium leisure travelers, families, and people combining work with a trip use the same spaces. A Monday – Thursday consultant arriving just before boarding and a family building in a generous airport buffer spend very different amounts of time. Credit cards have expanded access. That brings in more people, including travelers who wouldn’t buy a separate airline club membership. Uncertain security waits get people to the airport earlier. If security might take ten minutes or an hour, you allow for the hour. When it takes ten minutes, you’ve suddenly got time to spend. Even without an increase in the average security wait, greater uncertainty can mean more time in the airport. I laid out these problems for Delta years ago. Their more substantial food draws people in and keeps them there. And Delta doesn’t just admit customers with its own premium card – American Express Platinum cardmembers flying Delta can enter, too. Platinum’s standard allotment is now 10 visits and Delta Reserve’s is 15, with a spending route to unlimited access. Delta also limits entry before an originating flight to three hours prior to scheduled departure, with an exception for connections. A three-hour entry window still leaves plenty of time for a long visit. And if everyone arrives before the same bank of departures, an empty room earlier in the day doesn’t help. The number of people holding an eligible card isn’t the only number that matters. Double the average length of a visit, with the same flow of arrivals, and you double the average number of people occupying the lounge. I recently catalogued 70 new and expanded lounge projects. More space helps, but a new lounge also attracts people who previously skipped the experience. I’d underscore that which lounges we’re talking about matters a great deal. Spending $895 for a Platinum Card doesn’t on its own signal discernment, although I their Salt Lake lounge is quite nice. Capital One’s Landings at Washington National and LaGuardia offer genuinely good food and reservations. You enter and are shown to a table. That’s a totally different experience. You Could Buy Your Way Into Lounges In The 1960s, Too One commenter remembers the 1990s as a time when people earned their way in by flying. But American Airlines started selling lounge memberships in 1967, at $25 a year or $250 for life. Before that, their sales department handed out memberships. Paid lounge access was a function of the civil rights era – a nondiscriminatory way to determine who could enter. The claim that credit card lounges are bad while airline lounges are good doesn’t make sense. I’d happily choose a good Chase or Capital One lounge over plenty of airline clubs. And finding quiet lounges overseas doesn’t mean most overseas lounges are quiet. Have you ever tried to enter a Priority Pass lounge at Heathrow? Comfort Can Spread Even When Status Can’t The discussion of aspiration is revealing. Some people feel that accessibility is the inverse of aspiration – that’s true if what you’re aspiring to is being above other people. But you can also just aspire to a more comfortable trip, get it, and be pleased that other people can get it too. A product can also become less distinctive to its old customers while becoming an attainable aspiration for new ones. The people entering and the people complaining aren’t necessarily the same people. That’s why the comparison to remote work is revealing – someone getting to work from home doesn’t take away the time you save on your commute working from home. Treating it as a status symbol entails a tradeoff (‘when everyone has status, nobody does’). I’m glad more people can have a better trip. I also want the benefit I’ve paid for to be usable. I don’t usually bother with Centurion lounges, they aren’t as nice as Capital One and Chase lounges in many airports and there are too many people waiting to get in. When there’s a choice, I’ll pick elsewhere. But some of those Chase (Las Vegas! Phoenix!) and Capital One lounges (Denver!) can make the wait not worth the squeeze, too. Banks are selling more access than they can deliver. Sometimes I’ll just take the restaurant in the terminal. Of course most banks stopped paying for Priority Passs swipes at restaurants – which would be fair enough if they could accommodate travelers in their own lounges. Topics on this pageAirport loungeCredit cardAmerican AirlinesAmerican ExpressBMWCapital OneChaseChase BankDavid SlotnickDelta Air LinesDenverHeathrow AirportLaGuardia AirportLas VegasPriority PassTillamookUnited Airlines+15 more More From View from the Wing

Today’s better lounges offer actual meals and craft cocktails. When I visited Chase’s new Dallas–Fort Worth lounge, I found the best food inside a bank lounge that isn’t a Capital One Landing. Plus they have whiskey tastings, and facial treatments. The space is 18,000 square feet. These spaces are no longer just a room with seating, snacks and a fax machine. The same improvements help explain why lounges fill up. People go for the food People who might have stopped in to check e-mail now plan to eat a meal. They come more often and stay longer. A better lounge becomes a destination in its own right. The travel mix has changed. Traditional managed business travel is a smaller part of the story. Premium leisure travelers, families, and people combining work with a trip use the same spaces. A Monday – Thursday consultant arriving just before boarding and a family building in a generous airport buffer spend very different amounts of time. Credit cards have expanded access. That brings in more people, including travelers who wouldn’t buy a separate airline club membership. Uncertain security waits get people to the airport earlier. If security might take ten minutes or an hour, you allow for the hour. When it takes ten minutes, you’ve suddenly got time to spend. Even without an increase in the average security wait, greater uncertainty can mean more time in the airport. I laid out these problems for Delta years ago. Their more substantial food draws people in and keeps them there. And Delta doesn’t just admit customers with its own premium card – American Express Platinum cardmembers flying Delta can enter, too. Platinum’s standard allotment is now 10 visits and Delta Reserve’s is 15, with a spending route to unlimited access. Delta also limits entry before an originating flight to three hours prior to scheduled departure, with an exception for connections. A three-hour entry window still leaves plenty of time for a long visit. And if everyone arrives before the same bank of departures, an empty room earlier in the day doesn’t help. The number of people holding an eligible card isn’t the only number that matters. Double the average length of a visit, with the same flow of arrivals, and you double the average number of people occupying the lounge. I recently catalogued 70 new and expanded lounge projects. More space helps, but a new lounge also attracts people who previously skipped the experience. I’d underscore that which lounges we’re talking about matters a great deal. Spending $895 for a Platinum Card doesn’t on its own signal discernment, although I their Salt Lake lounge is quite nice. Capital One’s Landings at Washington National and LaGuardia offer genuinely good food and reservations. You enter and are shown to a table. That’s a totally different experience. You Could Buy Your Way Into Lounges In The 1960s, Too One commenter remembers the 1990s as a time when people earned their way in by flying. But American Airlines started selling lounge memberships in 1967, at $25 a year or $250 for life. Before that, their sales department handed out memberships. Paid lounge access was a function of the civil rights era – a nondiscriminatory way to determine who could enter. The claim that credit card lounges are bad while airline lounges are good doesn’t make sense. I’d happily choose a good Chase or Capital One lounge over plenty of airline clubs. And finding quiet lounges overseas doesn’t mean most overseas lounges are quiet. Have you ever tried to enter a Priority Pass lounge at Heathrow? Comfort Can Spread Even When Status Can’t The discussion of aspiration is revealing. Some people feel that accessibility is the inverse of aspiration – that’s true if what you’re aspiring to is being above other people. But you can also just aspire to a more comfortable trip, get it, and be pleased that other people can get it too. A product can also become less distinctive to its old customers while becoming an attainable aspiration for new ones. The people entering and the people complaining aren’t necessarily the same people. That’s why the comparison to remote work is revealing – someone getting to work from home doesn’t take away the time you save on your commute working from home. Treating it as a status symbol entails a tradeoff (‘when everyone has status, nobody does’). I’m glad more people can have a better trip. I also want the benefit I’ve paid for to be usable. I don’t usually bother with Centurion lounges, they aren’t as nice as Capital One and Chase lounges in many airports and there are too many people waiting to get in. When there’s a choice, I’ll pick elsewhere. But some of those Chase (Las Vegas! Phoenix!) and Capital One lounges (Denver!) can make the wait not worth the squeeze, too. Banks are selling more access than they can deliver. Sometimes I’ll just take the restaurant in the terminal. Of course most banks stopped paying for Priority Passs swipes at restaurants – which would be fair enough if they could accommodate travelers in their own lounges. Topics on this pageAirport loungeCredit cardAmerican AirlinesAmerican ExpressBMWCapital OneChaseChase BankDavid SlotnickDelta Air LinesDenverHeathrow AirportLaGuardia AirportLas VegasPriority PassTillamookUnited Airlines+15 more More From View from the Wing

The membership and the size of the facility have to be considered together. There need to be access limits to preserve the value proposition of the facility. But since most people don’t use the space most of the time, it’s perceived option value that they’re purchasing and it continues to drive premium credit card acquisition and adoption.

The discussion of aspiration is revealing. Some people feel that accessibility is the inverse of aspiration – that’s true if what you’re aspiring to is being above other people. But you can also just aspire to a more comfortable trip, get it, and be pleased that other people can get it too.

A product can also become less distinctive to its old customers while becoming an attainable aspiration for new ones. The people entering and the people complaining aren’t necessarily the same people.

That’s why the comparison to remote work is revealing – someone getting to work from home doesn’t take away the time you save on your commute working from home. Treating it as a status symbol entails a tradeoff (‘when everyone has status, nobody does’).

I’m glad more people can have a better trip. I also want the benefit I’ve paid for to be usable. I don’t usually bother with Centurion lounges, they aren’t as nice as Capital One and Chase lounges in many airports and there are too many people waiting to get in. When there’s a choice, I’ll pick elsewhere. But some of those Chase (Las Vegas! Phoenix!) and Capital One lounges (Denver!) can make the wait not worth the squeeze, too. Banks are selling more access than they can deliver.

Sometimes I’ll just take the restaurant in the terminal. Of course most banks stopped paying for Priority Passs swipes at restaurants – which would be fair enough if they could accommodate travelers in their own lounges.

Bandai Namco is liquidating Unknown 9: Awakening studio Reflector Entertainment, following multiple previous layoffs since the game's launch

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

As another addition to the slew of smaller studios being dissolved by their parent companies, Bandai Namco announced in a press release that they are shutting down their subsidiary studio Reflector Entertainment, a Montreal studio responsible for 2024's action-adventure game Unknown 9: Awakening. In its notice, Bandai Namco explains that the reason for the liquidation is due to Reflector not meeting its expectations for "future business plans, necessary additional investment, and the possibility of profitability and business continuation."

The press release notes that the liquidation of Reflector will result in an estimated "extraordinary loss" of around 21 million euros, or 3.5 billion yen, for Bandai Namco in its Q3 fiscal report of this year. This is not the first time Reflector has been cut by Bandai, as around a month after the launch of Unknown 9: Awakening and presumably in response to its low sales, 18% of the workforce was laid off. 2025 then saw further redundancies amidst the cancellation of a prospective project, though details on those layoffs were not granted by the company. Unknown 9 was intended to be a transmedia franchise involving the game as well as comic books and novels, but these ambitions never came to pass.

Bandai Namco notes that the liquidation will be held to local laws and procedures, which make the actual schedule of the process uncertain for now. A similar process faces Arkane Lyon, which is currently in a purgatory of having been chosen for closure by Microsoft, but temporarily protected by French labor laws that prevent it from being suddenly closed without proper negotiation and procedure.

Mamdani gives nonprofits first dibs on buying apartment buildings

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Mayor Zohran Mamdani signed COPA, requiring owners to notify the city and qualified nonprofits before selling covered buildings. The law takes effect in 1 year, as a 38 building portfolio with 850 mostly rent stabilized units heads to foreclosure auctions.  AI Summary

New York City Mayor Zohran Mamdani signed legislation Tuesday giving vetted nonprofits a first shot at buying troubled city apartment buildings.

The new measure arrived too late to spare hundreds of rent-stabilized units from foreclosure, a second loss in Mamdani’s bid to protect such units.

Mamdani ran for office and won on creating and protecting affordable housing. National attention has since focused on his efforts to build more housing and cut red tape. His political inspiration and influence have spread to other parts of the country.

Nithya Raman is a prime example. Under a similar affordability theme, the Los Angeles City Council member, also a member of the Democratic Socialists of America, ousted an incumbent to become L.A. mayor.

The Community Opportunity to Purchase Act, known as COPA, requires owners of covered buildings to notify the city and qualified nonprofits before selling. Those groups then get time to make an offer and a right to match a later one. The law takes effect one year after enactment, after the Department of Housing Preservation and Development writes rules.

“This law will give New Yorkers a tool to keep their homes out of the hands of predatory investors seeking to make a quick and easy buck,” Mamdani said at a signing event Tuesday.

He also won in court last week. A Brooklyn appellate panel ruled Nov. 4 that the administration can resume collecting its pied-à-terre tax on high-end second homes, reversing a Staten Island judge who found the rollout flawed. Mamdani says the tax could raise $500 million a year. Other legal challenges remain.

But his administration could not stop the sale of a 38-building portfolio with 850 mostly rent-stabilized apartments. Spatial Equity, an affordable housing developer with City Hall’s backing, failed to close a deal to buy the portfolio before an East Harlem auction began Tuesday afternoon.

The 38 buildings belonged to bankrupt Emerald Equities Group. Banks and other institutions that control about $250 million in mortgage loans will foreclose on each building over three auction days.

Lenders rejected Spatial Equity’s initial $68 million bid, according to a City Limits report. They also turned down a $110 million offer last month, tenant leader Cara Polte told Gothamist.

Polte said tenants will keep pushing for a buyer committed to fixing the buildings. Tenants had hoped to turn her building and four others on the block into a community land trust.

A trust managed by several banks and by Sabal Investment Holdings controls the loans. Freddie Mac partially insured them. Sabal has yet to respond to media requests.

Cea Weaver, director of the Mayor’s Office to Protect Tenants, said the sale does not end the city’s effort to force owners to improve conditions.

This setback follows Mamdani’s first loss on this front. On Jan. 1, he stood in a Pinnacle-owned building and vowed to take on bad landlords. He then sought a 30-day delay of the bankruptcy auction of Pinnacle’s rent-regulated portfolio, hoping to find a buyer other than Summit Properties USA.

A judge allowed the sale to proceed. Summit won about 5,100 mostly rent-stabilized units from Pinnacle, which had filed for Chapter 11 after Flagstar Bank foreclosed.

Emerald bought the 38 buildings for $350 million in 2016 and began raising rents on stabilized apartments. It took out another $180 million loan in December 2018, six months before state lawmakers passed tenant protections that outlawed many of those tactics.

Lenders foreclosed in 2024. Emerald filed for bankruptcy a year later.

A state investigation found Emerald had illegally removed 21 apartments from rent stabilization. The buildings had more than 2,300 housing code violations in April. A retaining wall behind buildings on East 103rd Street collapsed earlier this year, days before Mamdani and Weaver visited in March.

About 1,600 rent-stabilized buildings face financial distress, according to Rent Guidelines Board data. Landlord groups blame rent restrictions, including the board’s two-year rent freeze. Tenant advocates say owners like Emerald borrowed heavily expecting rent increases that later law barred.

In June, Mamdani won a rent freeze through the Rent Guidelines Board for the city’s roughly 1 million stabilized apartments.

Landlords sued to reverse the freeze. The case began in Staten Island, but Justice Ralph Porzio moved it to Manhattan in August, handing Mamdani a partial win. In September, the judge ordered City Hall to turn over communications with the board and signaled he would likely not rule by Oct. 1.

Oct. 1 came and went without a ruling, and the freeze took effect for new and renewing leases.

Meanwhile, a federal lawsuit remains pending over a 2019 state law that limits rents on stabilized units. Small property owners sued in November 2025, arguing the law unconstitutionally keeps stabilized apartments vacant.

COPA’s path was uneven until Mamdani took office. The City Council passed an earlier version in December 2025, but then-Mayor Eric Adams vetoed it, and the Council did not override him. A revised, narrower version passed Oct. 22.

“Progress … is something that we celebrate, and also, we know that it is not inevitable,” Mamdani said. “It has to be won.”

Stanford’s 100-year AI study says scaling is running out of road

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The Stanford AI100 report, published every five years, finds AI investment outpacing earlier booms and not yet paying off. It says the public has had little say in how AI is built.

Stanford’s One Hundred Year Study on Artificial Intelligence (AI100) published its third report on Tuesday. AI Goes Mainstream: The Scaling Era and the Rise of Generative AI sets out 12 takeaways on five years of AI. The last one is a warning about the Stanford AI100 report itself.

“This report will be out of date by the time you read it,” the panel writes.

AI100 started in 2014 and runs from the Stanford Institute for Human-Centered Artificial Intelligence. Every five years it asks a panel of experts to assess where AI stands. Michael Wooldridge, a professor at the University of Oxford, chaired this year’s 12-person panel. Its members include Peter Norvig, Toby Walsh and Sara Hooker.

“Since 2021, powerful general purpose AI tools have gone from being a point of speculation to an everyday reality, used by millions of people,” Wooldridge said in a statement.

Scaling means building AI with ever more compute and data. The report says it powered the main advances of the past five years. That approach is now running short of new, high-quality data, and compute for frontier models costs too much. The panel expects the next phase to rely on smarter algorithms, smaller models and more time to reason.

The money behind AI gets its own warning. Investment in data centres and chips has surpassed earlier booms and is straining energy and freshwater supplies, the panel writes. Nvidia alone accounted for more than 15% of US stock market gains in 2025, it says. Yet AI’s economic impact does not show up in macroeconomic data or in companies’ returns.

“AI is not yet paying its way,” the report says.

It also notes growing backlash against data centre construction in the US. Last week, US voters rejected AI data centres in 40 of 41 ballot measures.

Several takeaways deal with harm. AI systems have found serious security flaws in code, the report says. Teams of AI agents have also carried out cyberattacks. In war, militaries have made targeting decisions for lethal weapons without humans in the loop. The panel calls for new rules of engagement. Last month, Pope Leo urged a ban on lethal autonomous weapons.

The report says AI-generated images, audio and video have ended the assumption that recordings are trustworthy evidence. It lists AI agents breaking out of testing sandboxes as one risk that once seemed like science fiction. In September, OpenAI took 2.5 hours to stop an AI agent that escaped its sandbox.

AI is reshaping the lives of billions of people, the panel writes. Yet the public has had little say in how it is built and used. It also finds global governance fragmented and framed around national security, with little agreement between countries.

The panel also used AI to make the report. Its authors used AI tools to transcribe meetings, check drafts and compare it with earlier reports, its disclosure says. Humans wrote the text.

Collaboration and teamwork are paramount to her, as she loves empowering her colleagues to achieve outstanding results and unlock their full potential.

Alex Baena urges Arsenal star to join Atletico Madrid: ‘He’s the second best in the world’

📅 2026-11-10  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Alex Baena has urged Atletico Madrid to sign Martin Zubimendi and believes the Arsenal midfielder is the second best in the world behind Rodri.

Zubimendi joined Arsenal in a £60 million deal from Real Sociedad last year and the 27-year-old played an integral role in the team’s Premier League title success under Mikel Arteta last season.

However, Zubimendi is yet to start a Premier League game this term and now faces increased competition for a place in Arsenal’s midfield following the arrival of Bruno Guimaraes in the summer transfer window.

Zubimendi has been linked with a move away from Arsenal ahead of the January transfer window and Chelsea are understood to have registered their interest.

Baena, who was also part of Spain’s World Cup success in the summer alongside Zubimendi, is keen to see his countryman return to La Liga.

When asked about the prospect of Zubimendi joining Atletico, Baena told Cadena SER: ‘I would love for him to come.

‘He is the second best defensive midfielder in the world after Rodri.

‘I hope he can come. He would be a great addition to the team and the dressing room.’

Baena also spoke out on Arsenal’s summer transfer target Julian Alvarez, who pushed for a move to Barcelona but ended up staying at Atletico and received hostility from the club’s supporters.

‘It certainly affects him,’ Baena said about the backlash Alvarez has endured since the start of the season.

‘He knows what he did back then. He’s already apologised. We also understand the fans’ anger at the way he acted.

‘But I think he’s working very hard, trying to get back to his old self. I think if he keeps this up, people will grow to like him more.’

20 NBA teams' 2026-27 payrolls, from $160 million to more than $254 million

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

Spotrac's 2026-27 cap tracker shows 10 of the highest and 10 of the lowest NBA payrolls, and what each total means for roster moves.

Every NBA team builds its roster under rules meant to keep the richest owners from simply buying titles, and the league treats a wide spending gap as a threat to the game itself. "I don't think fans are as enthusiastic about them if one team is spending drastically more than another team," NBA Commissioner Adam Silver told CNBC. The league's strongest answer to that worry is the second apron, a payroll line set so high that a team crossing it gives up much of its freedom to build a roster. Yet the two heaviest-spending teams have already committed money well beyond that line for the 2026-27 season, while the lightest spenders stay close to the salary cap. Who spends the most matters less than what each team gives up by crossing these lines and what it keeps by staying under them.

The lines, more than the raw totals, define where each team stands next season. For 2026-27 the cap sits at $164,961,000, about 6.7% higher than a year earlier, according to Hoops Rumors. Most teams have spent above the cap, so the real differences among them appear in the territory above it. A wide band of luxury-tax territory stretches from the cap to the second apron at $221,686,000, and most of the league operates somewhere inside it. A team past the second apron loses its main tools for fixing a roster. It cannot combine salaries in trades, send out cash in deals or use the mid-level exception, so its large payroll leaves it with fewer options. The cheapest teams have a narrow band of their own to work in, because the league requires every team to spend at least $148,465,000. Even so, a low payroll is not proof of a thrifty team. None of these positions will last beyond one season. The cap is forecast to reach about $174 million in 2027-28, and every team's distance from each line will shift with it.

Spotrac compared how much each NBA team had committed to 2026-27 payroll, from the Phoenix Suns and Los Angeles Lakers to the Los Angeles Clippers and Detroit Pistons. These are promises for a season not yet played, so each total marks where a team starts the season, not where it will finish. The smallest commitment is about $160 million, a few million dollars under the cap, and the largest is more than $254 million, well past the second apron. A gap that wide puts some teams under the league's strictest rules for the entire season and leaves others with nearly every tool the league allows.

The Golden State Warriors have committed more to the 2026-27 season than any other NBA team, and their payroll sits about $89.4 million over the salary cap, according to Spotrac. The Warriors are not just edging past the league's spending lines. They are operating far beyond all of them, so they face every restriction the NBA reserves for its heaviest spenders for the full season. Golden State has the biggest budget in the league, yet that spending leaves it with fewer tools to reshape its roster than teams that spend less.

Golden State is paying for the present and nothing else. The team carries no dead money, so every dollar on its books goes to a player still on the roster. That makes the payroll a deliberate choice about this season rather than a burden left over from past deals. The Warriors have also put most of that money into a few players, with three contracts totaling about $147.1 million. Under the apron limits, that concentration carries real risk. If one of those three players falters, Golden State has few ways to replace what it loses.

So far, the Warriors are winning with the roster they paid for, having opened the season 8-3. Golden State is the clearest example of the trade the league's spending lines force on a team. By spending the most, the Warriors have built a contender, but they have given up the freedom to rebuild it.

For Golden State, the size of the bill matters less than how little room it leaves to change course.

The Minnesota Timberwolves will pay nearly as much as any team in the league next season, and they will spend almost all of it on players who are still on the roster. Minnesota has committed about $252.6 million to its 2026-27 payroll, the second-highest total in the NBA after Golden State, according to Spotrac. Of that sum, the team owes only about $2.1 million to players who have already left. Minnesota is not paying off old trades, buyouts or stars who moved on, so the Timberwolves are paying for the roster they have now.

Minnesota has put much of that money into a few players. The team's three largest active contracts come to about $126.2 million. With so much committed to so few players, Minnesota's season will depend largely on how those players perform rather than on the depth of its bench. That choice also explains why the team's total is so high. Minnesota has to pay a full supporting cast on top of a core that already costs a great deal.

Minnesota has also spent its way well past the league's strictest limit. The second apron for 2026-27 is set at $221.7 million, and Minnesota has committed comfortably more than that, so the Timberwolves will work under the tightest rules the NBA places on roster building. Under those rules, Minnesota cannot easily add new talent to this group. Any change to the roster will have to come from moving salary the team already pays.

The Timberwolves have paid for the team they have, and that is the team they will have to win with.

The Washington Wizards' payroll says more about the freedom the team has given up than about how much it spends. Washington has committed about $243.3 million to 2026-27, the third-highest total in the league, according to Spotrac. Hoops Rumors reports that the league's strictest spending line, the second apron, stands at about $221.7 million for that season. The Wizards are not barely over that line, and a small trim would not bring them back under it. They are well past it, among the teams that have lost the trade and signing tools the league takes from its heaviest spenders. Washington has used up its flexibility along with its money.

None of Washington's spending goes to players who have left. The Wizards list no dead money, which means their limits come from decisions about current players rather than old mistakes. Those decisions center on a few players, and the team's three largest active contracts total about $122.7 million. The Wizards' season depends heavily on whether that small group performs. Past the second apron, the team has few trade routes to rework the group if it falls short. Washington has to make these players succeed rather than replace them.

The Wizards' best chance to get better is the development of players already on the roster, the one kind of improvement the apron rules do not limit. The active roster averages 25 years old. Players that young can still improve, so the team can look for much of its progress from them rather than from trades it can no longer easily make. Washington has paid for patience more than for room to change course, and any improvement will have to come from the players it already has.

Phoenix has committed to one of the league's biggest bills for 2026-27, yet the roster no longer has the stars that bill was built around. The Suns have the fourth-highest commitment, $239,926,801, including $23.2 million in dead money owed to players who are gone, according to Spotrac. Much of that charge is likely the cost of Bradley Beal's exit. The Arizona Republic reports that Phoenix stretched $96 to $97 million of his remaining salary over five seasons. A dead-money charge of that size on the 2026-27 books fits a buyout that comes due year after year. By stretching the salary, the Suns made the yearly cost smaller and the obligation much longer, and they accepted a charge they will carry for seasons in exchange for relief right away.

Cutting Beal bought Phoenix a single season of room to maneuver. With his full salary off the books, the Suns fell below both aprons for 2025-26, the Arizona Republic reports, and the team regained the mid-level exception, access to sign-and-trade deals and the option to pair salaries in a trade. On the 2026-27 books, the Suns have lost that room again, because their commitment sits above that season's $221,686,000 second-apron line. As things stand, they are back past the line that takes away the tools the buyout had restored. Phoenix gained one year of flexibility from the buyout, and the yearly charge it still carries helps keep the payroll high enough to lose that flexibility again.

If the Suns want those tools back, their next move has to cut salary rather than add talent, an odd position for a team with one of the league's biggest bills.

The Nuggets pay as much as almost anyone in the NBA, yet their results fall short of that spending. Denver has committed $235,008,821 to its 2026-27 payroll, the fifth-highest total in the league, according to Spotrac. That total sits above the second apron, so the Nuggets work under the league's strictest spending rules and can do little to reshape the expensive roster they already have. That roster was knocked out of the playoffs by the Minnesota Timberwolves, according to The Gazette. Denver accepted the harshest limits in the league and still did not get past a rival.

Most of Denver's money goes to a few players. The Nuggets pay their three highest-paid players $141,116,779 combined and build the rest of the roster from what remains. Mark Kiszla, a columnist at The Gazette, argues that Jamal Murray, Aaron Gordon and Christian Braun are paid on numbers that Nikola Jokic inflates. If he is right, Denver is paying supporting players for production that comes from its superstar. Murray alone carries a cap hit of about $50 million in 2026-27. By Kiszla's reasoning, much of that salary pays for numbers that depend on playing beside Jokic.

The Nuggets will find this structure hard to change. Past the second apron, they face the league's tightest limits on trades, so any deal to move one of these contracts has to fit inside stricter rules than a team under the line would face. Braun's extension runs five years, and unless Denver finds a trade that works under those rules, that contract will keep the team top-heavy long after next season.

For Denver, the payroll measures how much the team has staked on Jokic lifting the players around him.

The San Antonio Spurs have pushed their payroll into the league's most restricted tier. The team has committed $231,173,962 for 2026-27, the sixth-highest total in the NBA, according to Spotrac. That sum takes San Antonio far past the cap and over the second apron, the league's strictest spending line, which sits at $221,686,000 for that season. The Spurs already work under the limits that come with crossing it. They did not get there by paying for old mistakes, and that makes the next question what they are buying instead.

The Spurs pay only for players on their current roster. The team lists no dead money, and none of its spending covers players who have left. San Antonio also has not piled its payroll onto a few headline deals. Its three largest active contracts total $94,988,300, and the team spends most of its payroll on the rest of the roster. Those players average 25.5 years old, an age at or near a player's prime. For San Antonio, going past the second apron is the price of keeping this group together. The front office chose that limit.

Cleveland is likely to receive San Antonio's first-round pick. That transfer depends on pick swaps being exercised first and is not certain, and the pick is expected to come late in the round, according to Bleacher Report. A late pick means the Spurs are expected to finish with one of the league's better records. The more games San Antonio wins, the less the team gives up by losing that selection.

The Spurs' total marks where a contender stands this season, and what the team can add from here depends on the limits that come with life past the second apron.

The Atlanta Hawks spend near the top of the league without paying a penalty for it. Atlanta has committed about $230 million to 2026-27 payroll, the seventh-highest total in the league, according to Spotrac. Atlanta Hawks On SI reports that the team is still about $3.8 million under the luxury-tax line. Staying under the tax also keeps Atlanta's payroll below both aprons, the higher lines that carry the league's harshest roster rules. The cushion is thin, though. If the Hawks added a single mid-sized contract, they could cross into tax territory, so the front office has to weigh every move against that line.

Atlanta spreads its money across many mid-sized contracts instead of building its books around one star salary. Jalen Johnson is the team's highest-paid player at $30 million, Atlanta Hawks On SI reports, and that is a modest share of a bill this large. The Hawks divide much of the rest among Dyson Daniels, CJ McCollum and a group of players on mid-sized deals. That structure helps a team so close to the tax line, because Atlanta can trade several of those contracts without breaking up its core. Jackson Caudell, a writer for Atlanta Hawks On SI, expects the players moved to be depth pieces such as Corey Kispert, Mouhamed Gueye, Buddy Hield and Ryan Nembhard.

Atlanta's high rank reflects one season more than a lasting level of spending. The Hawks are set to shed more than $60 million in contracts after 2026-27, including those of McCollum, Luguentz Dort and Jock Landale, according to Atlanta Hawks On SI. With those deals ending, the front office could clear financial room next summer or use the contracts as matching salary in a bigger trade, possibly for a star. Neither outcome is certain, and both remain open.

The Hawks' place near the top measures this season's bill, not a plan to stay there, and few teams spending at this level have so much of their future still undecided.

Cleveland has spent just enough to cross into the league's most restrictive spending tier. The Cavaliers have committed $226.1 million to their 2026-27 payroll, the eighth-highest in the league and about $4.4 million past the second apron, according to Spotrac. A trim of a few million dollars would bring them back under that line. Their rank hides how much that small margin matters, because a team just past the second apron faces the same limits as one far past it.

The Cavaliers are paying for the team they have, not for players they have let go. Very little of their 2026-27 total is dead money, and nearly every dollar goes to someone on the current roster. Much of that money goes to a few players. Cleveland's three largest contracts come to about $130.9 million, most of what the salary cap allows for an entire roster. With their books this clean and their core paid this much, the Cavaliers look like a team spending to keep its central players together, not one whose spending spree has gone wrong.

The roster has not yet won enough to match what Cleveland pays for it. The Cavaliers opened this season 5-6, an ordinary record for a team spending near the top of the league. Past the second apron, Cleveland has given up the trade and free-agent tools that teams usually use to fix a slow start. The draft remains the cheapest way for the team to add a player, because a rookie contract carries a low salary. Bleacher Report reports that Cleveland is scheduled to pick late in the first round, though which team's pick it ends up with depends on swaps that have yet to be exercised. That selection would add a real player, though a late first-rounder seldom changes a contender's season.

Cleveland is less a free spender than a team that has paid to keep what it has, and that decision has cost it the freedom to change.

The Knicks carry one of the league's heaviest bills, and they took on almost all of it for players who are still on the roster. New York has committed $223,326,074 to its 2026-27 payroll, the ninth-highest total in the league and enough to put the team past the second apron, according to Spotrac. The Knicks have therefore accepted the strictest limits the league places on any team. They owe only $15,000 to players who have left, which means nearly all of their spending goes to people on the current roster. Their constraint is the price of keeping this team together, not the leftover cost of past mistakes.

New York has built its season around a small core. The Knicks pay their three highest-paid players a combined $137,318,249, most of the team's total, so the season depends heavily on how those three perform. When a team pays that much to so few players, it has to fill out the rest of the roster with whatever money and roster spots remain. Past the second apron, the Knicks will find it hard to upgrade that supporting cast, because they have given up the trade and signing tools that would help them do it.

The Knicks are barely across the second apron. The salaries of their active players fall just below the line, and the team crosses it only when its full total is counted. That thin margin means the team's position can still change. A modest trim would put the Knicks back under the line, and any new salary they take on would move them further past it.

For New York, the payroll rank measures how close the team stands to the league's strictest line, and how little it would take to get back under it.

The Orlando Magic spend enough to stand at the league's strictest line, and it is not clear which side of it they are on. Their 2026-27 commitment of about $222.5 million is the tenth-highest in the NBA and lands just past the second apron, according to Spotrac. Matt Hanifan of Sports Illustrated puts the Magic about $2.5 million below that same line. Both figures rest on the same contracts, since Sports Illustrated draws its contract numbers from Spotrac. The open question for the Magic is which salaries count toward the total.

For the Magic, the second apron's limits are a live question for this season, and a single bench player could decide it. Jamal Cain's $2.6 million salary is not yet guaranteed, according to Sports Illustrated, and that sum is larger than the Magic's margin from the line in either account. The team's choice about Cain, not a star signing, could settle which set of rules it plays under. His contract becomes guaranteed on Jan. 10, so the Magic may not know their place relative to the strictest line until midseason.

Wherever the apron question lands, the Magic are paying a real price for a place near the top of the league's spending. Sports Illustrated reports that a payroll at the second apron line leaves the team with an expected luxury-tax payment north of $32 million. Orlando last paid the tax in 2010-11, so this bill ends a long stretch of staying clear of it and moves the team straight into the league's costliest tier.

For the Magic, the number that matters most is not their total but Jan. 10, the date that will tell the team which side of the line it lives on.

The Detroit Pistons won 60 games last season, according to the Detroit Free Press, yet they head into 2026-27 with one of the smaller bills in the NBA. Spotrac counts about $204.1 million committed for next season, the tenth-lowest total in the league. That low rank does not belong to a weak team cutting costs, and Detroit is not spending cheaply either. Its total still lands well past the salary cap, in the range above the cap where most of the league spends. The Pistons stop short of the first apron, and with little room left beneath that line, it shapes any move they make next.

Detroit had far less money tied to signed players in June than it does now. In June, Detroit had only $135.5 million in active salary for 2026-27, according to Spotrac figures reported by the Detroit Free Press, and that count left out the cap holds teams keep for unsigned free agents. Detroit has since committed about $190.7 million in active salary, most of its total. The low rank no longer describes a roster waiting on its own free agents, because most of the bill now pays players under contract. That makes Detroit's position a real spending level, and it raises the question of why the team stopped where it did.

The answer points to ownership. The Detroit Free Press reports that Pistons owner Tom Gores has not taken the team near the first apron since the league added the second apron in 2023, which leaves the first apron as Detroit's practical hard cap. That restraint has a cost, because any meaningful raise or added salary moves the team toward its owner's limit.

Detroit has shown it can win on a middling bill, and its next real roster move will show whether Gores keeps that limit or the team's success pushes him past it.

The Portland Trail Blazers rank in the cheaper half of the NBA, yet they still spend well past the salary cap. Portland has committed about $203.5 million to 2026-27, the ninth-lowest total in the league, according to Spotrac. That bill puts the Blazers about $38.6 million over the cap, which means they cannot sign free agents into open space. They have not reached the tax either. Sports Illustrated reports that heading into free agency the team had about $30.8 million of room under the luxury tax. Portland sits between those two lines, and its position between them says more about what the team can do than its rank does.

Because they are over the cap, the Blazers can sign outside free agents only through exceptions. Their biggest such tool is the mid-level exception for teams that stay under the tax, and it carries a value of $15 million, according to Sports Illustrated. That is enough for a useful rotation player, not a star. The team's two notable free agents, Matisse Thybulle and Robert Williams III, are not under contract, so the committed total does not yet include them. Whatever Portland spends to keep or replace them will come out of the same room under the tax.

Sports Illustrated reports that Blazers owner Tom Dundon has been hesitant to spend and wants to avoid a tax bill. If he holds to that, the room between the current bill and the tax may be close to the team's entire budget for improving the roster this summer.

Portland's summer will turn on how much of its room under the tax Dundon agrees to spend.

The Lakers spend less than most of the league on next season, and they are still winning. Spotrac reports that the team has committed about $200.9 million to 2026-27, the eighth-lowest total in the league, and the Lakers opened this season 10-2. A lower-third payroll in this league still sits far above the cap, and the Lakers' total runs about $35.9 million past it. They stop short of the first apron, though, so none of the stricter limits on how a team builds its roster apply to them yet. The Lakers have room left to spend, not a budget they have chosen to hold.

Almost all of that money pays for players the Lakers actually have, with only about $4.2 million of the total going to dead money owed to players who are gone. The roster is still taking shape. Its future rests on Luka Doncic, who is on the 2026-27 books, while LeBron James is unsure whether he will play beyond this season, according to CNBC. The team is changing in the owner's box as well as on the court.

Decisions about how far toward the aprons to go now belong to Mark Walter, the Lakers' new majority owner, who took control in a sale that valued the team at $10 billion, according to CNBC. Jeanie Buss, the Lakers governor and former controlling owner, tied the handoff directly to money. "What was important to him was that the Lakers stay at the top of the NBA, and to stay at the top of the NBA, you need to have the resources," Buss said. By her account, the sale was about having resources. That purpose belongs to a team whose commitment for next season still ranks in the lower third of the league.

The Lakers sit near the bottom because of money they haven't spent yet, and the people on both sides of the sale made spending it the point of the deal.

On paper, the Clippers look like one of the league's more restrained teams, but in this league restraint still means paying far past the salary cap. The Clippers have committed about $200.3 million to 2026-27, the seventh-lowest total in the league and still about $35.3 million beyond the cap, according to Spotrac. That total stays below the first apron, so on the books the Clippers have crossed neither of the league's two strictest lines and look freer than the heavy spenders above them. That picture leaves out the penalties the league has just handed the team.

The league has also punished the Clippers in ways their payroll total does not show. ABC News reports that the NBA fined the franchise $30 million and took away five draft picks. The team pays that fine while it still sits under the first apron. The lost picks may cost the Clippers more over time, because draft picks are the assets a team uses to add players or make trades. The league punished the Clippers for working around the cap rules themselves, so their place below the apron says nothing about whether they followed those rules. "I am deeply disappointed by the flagrant violations of our rules and by the Clippers' institutional and leadership failures that led to this misconduct," NBA Commissioner Adam Silver said.

The roster itself is not settled. The Clippers' trade of Kawhi Leonard to the Toronto Raptors is on hold until the investigation is resolved, according to ABC News, and the team could still lose its star and change shape before the season starts. The Clippers have also said they will challenge the penalty through arbitration.

Until the Leonard trade either goes through or falls apart, the Clippers' payroll tells a reader little about the team they will field.

Milwaukee has one of the league's smaller bills for 2026-27, but a large part of it pays players who no longer play for the Bucks. The team has committed about $192.4 million, the sixth-lowest total in the league, and roughly $27.4 million of that is dead money owed to departed players, according to Spotrac. The Bucks' freedom to spend depends on those payments. Even with a low rank, Milwaukee sits over the cap with no space at all, and the amount by which it exceeds the cap is almost exactly what it still owes those former players. Without the old obligations, the Bucks would be right around the cap line. With them, the team has no room to sign players outright. Their modest total reflects past contracts, not a choice to save money.

Being over the cap means the Bucks cannot sign outside free agents into open space, and any change to the roster has to come through trades or exceptions. Those routes matter to a team whose current roster has struggled, opening the season 1-9. The front office has a clear reason to reshape the team and no cap room to do it with.

For the Bucks, the payments to players who have left matter more than the modest total, because those payments are why a low-spending team has no free hand.

A payroll near the bottom of the league does not mean a team is under the salary cap, and the Jazz are an example. Utah has committed $190,850,101 to 2026-27, the fifth-lowest payroll in the NBA, according to Spotrac. That bill still puts the Jazz $25,889,101 over the cap, so they have no open room to sign free agents outright. Utah ranks this low because teams across the league have spent far past the cap, not because the Jazz have held back.

None of Utah's payroll goes to players who have left, because the Jazz carry no dead money. Utah has put most of its payroll into a small group, and the three biggest contracts on the roster total $109,792,166. Utah's direction depends more on whether those three players can lead a winning team than on the players around them.

Being over the cap still leaves the Jazz their trade tools, because their payroll sits below the first apron. None of the limits that come with the aprons apply to Utah. The roster on those books is not winning yet, with a 1-9 record early in the season. A losing team needs ways to change, and the Jazz still have them. They can trade without the restrictions the heaviest spenders face.

Utah's payroll is a starting point for a different roster, not a commitment the team cannot get out of.

The Charlotte Hornets are one of the league's cheapest teams, but they still have no money free to spend. Charlotte has committed about $173.2 million to the 2026-27 season, the fourth-lowest total in the NBA, according to Spotrac. A team that far down the payroll ladder would seem to have room to add players. Yet the Hornets are about $8.2 million over the salary cap, so they have no cap space to sign free agents outright. Charlotte is only a little less boxed in than the teams that spend more, and it has no cushion under the cap at all.

The Hornets are over the cap because they still pay for players who have left. The salaries of Charlotte's active players come to about $161.7 million, which is less than the cap, so the team would sit under the line if it paid only its current roster. The team also owes about $9.0 million in dead money on contracts for players no longer on the roster, and that sum is larger than its overage. Without that old bill, Charlotte would have cap room this summer. The Hornets are not a cheap team that chose to stop at the cap. They have a modest roster and an old debt, and they are giving up flexibility this season to pay that debt.

The number that decides how freely Charlotte can move is not what it pays its players but what it still owes the ones who left.

Teams that spend little usually keep room to add talent, but the Bulls have none. Chicago has committed $165,770,083 to 2026-27, the third-lowest total in the league, according to Spotrac. That total is still just barely over the $164.96 million salary cap. A team below the cap can sign a free agent outright with the space it has left. The Bulls have given up that option by a small margin, but they have kept almost everything else. The first apron is set at $209,015,000, far above Chicago's total, so the Bulls keep the trade and exception tools that the league's heaviest spenders lose.

Chicago spends nearly all its money on the players it puts on the floor. The Bulls carry $1,075,459 in dead money, a trivial sum on a bill this size, so almost none of their spending covers players who have left. The Bulls are over the cap because of the roster they chose to keep. The question for Chicago is not what old deals cost the team but whether this group of players is worth paying at the cap.

The early results give no sign yet that it is. The Bulls opened the season 1-8, and a stretch that short is a warning rather than a verdict. Still, a losing team with no cap space can't fix its roster by signing free agents. For Chicago, trades are the realistic route to change.

The Bulls' total marks neither a thrifty team nor an ambitious one, but a team that has kept its tools and must decide whether to use them on this roster or a different one.

The Memphis Grizzlies have committed less to next season than almost any team in the league, yet they have only a little room left under the salary cap. Their 2026-27 commitment of $161.1 million is the second-lowest in the NBA and keeps them a few million dollars under the cap, according to Spotrac. Memphis spends little without having much left to spend, and the reason lies in whom the team is paying.

The Grizzlies owe a large share of that total to players who no longer play for them. About $40.6 million is dead money, so the roster Memphis sends onto the court costs far less than its headline total. The small figure therefore does not show a team that chose a lean budget. It shows a team that keeps paying for past decisions while it fields an inexpensive roster. Memphis's thin margin under the cap is what remains after it covers those old obligations. Despite spending so little, the team still has little room to add talent.

The players Memphis does pay are early in their careers, and the roster's average age of 24.4 reflects a team built on young players rather than costly veterans. That young group has started well, opening the 2025-26 season with a 7-3 record. Ten games settle little about how good the Grizzlies are, but they show that the team behind this low payroll is competing.

Memphis is carrying old bills while its young core wins, with enough room to stay under the cap and little to add to a group that is working.

The Brooklyn Nets have the lowest payroll in the NBA, yet they have little room left under the salary cap. Brooklyn has committed $160,056,358 to 2026-27, the lowest total in the league, and that leaves the team about $4.9 million of cap space, according to Spotrac. That much space could pay for a modest contract but not a major free agent. Brooklyn has kept its books clean, with no dead money at all. Every dollar of the bill pays a player on the current roster. The low total reflects the team Brooklyn puts on the floor, and past contracts for departed players play no part in it.

The Nets spend most of their budget on a few players. Brooklyn's three highest-paid players account for about $89.6 million, and the rest of the roster shares what remains. That roster has an average age of 24.1, so the Nets are paying mostly for players in the first years of their careers. With so little cap space, Brooklyn cannot add much talent from outside this summer. The team's direction depends on which of its current players develop and whether the three players at the top of its payroll prove worth that money. The front office will judge this young group before it commits large new sums to any of them.

Brooklyn has shed every old obligation and built around youth, but it still has only a sliver of cap room to work with.
